STRAIGHT TO THE LUNGS.

New and Up-to-Date Treatment for Cough s, Colds, and Bronchitis.

Most people have enly too goed reason to dread the rapid apprcach of winter, with its fog and frost, snow and sleet, cutting winds and clammy atmosphere. Most of us have suffered from bad colds, winter coughs, tender throats, or sore chests, to say netting of the fear of pleurisy, pneumonia, bronchitis and asthma. But Peps, the wonderful medicine compressed into solid tablet fotrn, have robbed winter of all these terrors. Peps are not only a sure cure for all throat and chest ailments, but a certain safe- guard against all those colds and chills which are the gateway of all threat and chest troubles. With a box of Peps always handy you may defy coughs, colds, and bronchitis, even in the worst weather. You take a Peps tablet out of the silver jacket in which it is sealed, and place it on thelongue. As the tablet dissolves, powerful healing, strengthening, and get m-destroying fumes arise, and are breathed direct into the throat, bronchial tubes, and lungs. In this Unique fashion the throat and chest are cleared of phlegm and other impurities, lurking germs of disease are destroyed, the membranes injured and inflamed by incessant coughing, are soothed and healed, and all the breathing passages strengthened and toned up. The uselessness of liquid remedies for lung troubles is obvious to all, since it is quite impos- sible to swallow a liquid into the lungs. All' liquids pass straight into the'stemach, and mcst old-fashioned cough mixtures and cough-cures are dangerously loaded v, ith opium, chloral, morphia, and other narcotics and sedatives. Peps are entirely free from these abominable drugs, and are safe alike for young and old. The Peps method of compressing a breathe-able medicine into solid tablet form is the latest triumph of modern science, and has realised the aim of many great specialists in throat and lung troubles.

Peps are praised everywhere by people who have proved their wonderful power in cases of chronic cough, colds, bronchitis, asthma, influenza, sore throat, winter cough, old age cough, bron- chial asthma, difficult breathing, tightness of the chest, laryngitis, quinsy, chest weakness, factory and workshop coughs, cli!dren's coughs, bronchial colds, wheezinesQ, huskiness, and other throat and chest ailments. . .

Firmly refuse all cheap substitutes, and look for the familiar blue and gold wrapper with the short distinctive title —Peps—in bold characters on the box and in raised letters on every tiblet. Of all chemists or post free from Peps, Cal lton Hill, Leeds, at Is. Ud. or 2s. 9d. per box.

SLATE CO. V. WARREN.

END IT LITIGATION.

Injunction as to S!2tES Oniy.

The case of Warrington Slate Co. v. 3: E. Warren came up for hearing at the Lancashire Chancery Court held at Manchester on Monday.—Mr. Eastwood (instructed by Mr. F. R. Roberta) was for the plaintiff company, and Mr. Eadford (instructed by Mr. C. H. Unsworth) for the defendant. Mr. Eastwood said this was a motion for judgment in an action brought by the Warrington Slate Company against a former emplo36. TLe plaintiffs asked that the defendant might be restrained for a period of one year and six calendar months from the 31st May, 1967, Item carrying on or being interested either solely or as a shareholder, partner, manager, lender' servant, adviser or otherwise in the business of a dealer in slates within the area included- in a circle within a 50 miles radius from Arpley Station, IVarrington, in the county of Lancaster, and for payment by the defendant to the plaintiffs of £lO damages, and for payment of the costs of this action down to the date of the defence, so far as they relate to the claim of the plaintiff com- pany, in respect of the dealings by the defendant in slates within the said ate, and the defendant may be ordered to pay the costs of the plaintiff company in that motion." His Honour asked what was the nature of the com- pany's business, and Mr. Eastwood replied th:.t it was a slate business. The firm also dealt in cement, coal, building materials and matters of that sort; but their main trade was con- nected with slates. The defendant, he understood, was willing to submit to an injunction restraining him from dealing in slates for a year and six calendar months. The only matter which had to ho decided that day was as to costs. Mr. Radford: But what are you going to do with the rest of the action relating to the other business ? Mr. Eastwood : I ask now for judgment on the facts have stated. If my friend submits to the injunc- tion with regard to the slates, that's all we want. His Honour: There is nothing about cement?

Mr. Eastwood : Oh, no. Mr. Radford argued that defendant ought not to pay all the costs of the action, but only such part as related to slates. There were several businesses in connection with the firm, but that connected mith slates was more extensive than the others. Some however were limited to the immediate area of Warrington. His Honour granted an injunction in the terms of the notice of motion and ordered the defendant to pay the costs of the action incut red in the claim as to slates only, together with RIO damages. The result of the action is that Mr. Warren will now be able to deal in everything except slates, but he is -restrained from trading in slates for fourteen months from the present time.

THE NEW WIRELESS SERVICE. AN INTERESTING MESSAGE.

One of the earliest messages to be carried by the ethereal waves on the opening of the trans-Atlantic wireless telegraph service was the one quoted below conveying cordial congratulation to Dr. Marconi from the London Office of the Columbia Phonograph Com- pany with whom the "wireless wizard" has been for seme time associated as Consulting Physicist. "The Columbia Phonograph Company hastens to greet M. Marconi, their Consulting Physicist, and to extend their heartiest congratulations upon his magnificent achievement ef what,even a decade ago, would have been regarded as the wildest dream of a chimerical visionary. Accustomed as this genera- tion is to the marvellous, the harnessing of Nature's forces to transmit, without physical connection, telegraphic messnes from one hemisphere to the other, overwhelms the mind by its mystery and magnitude. Your name, associated with this stupendous accomplishment, will never be forgotten to the end of time." It is interesting to recall the opinion expressed by Marconi, on joining the Columbia staff, after his examination of the plant and product of the Company. In a press interview at the time, he made the signi- ficant remark :—" So far as the mechanical construction of the Columbia Graph-o-phone is concerned, I do not see how it can be improved." Full particulars of the instrument which thus carries the recommendation of the most talked of man of the day may he had free by simply dropping a postcard to " Room 20," Columbia Phonograph Company, General,64 and 66 Oxford-street, London, W.

NEWTON-LE-WILLOW'S ARGUMENT.

It is always helpful to find others arguing on the same side RS ourselves. here is fine support from Newton-le-Willows of an argument that many of us here have used. This is what Newton-le-Willows says. "I was troubled with inflammation of the bladder, and backache, for six years," says Mrs. M. A. Chadwick, 41, Mill-lane, Newton-le-Willows ; "a bad confinement caused the ailments, and from then I suffered off and on. Every cold I caught, or any extra work I did, brought on the troubles ; sometimes I would ta laid up, once being in bed a fortnight. " The pains in my hack struck ra like a knife-thrust about every twenty ruhnites, or if 1 moved suddenly : there was a constant pain as well. I used to apply warm flannels, which eased mesomewhpt. The urinary secretions were rather thick, and very painful. "I always felt very wearied and drowsy, and also nervous. I had headaches' giddinesg, and terrible palpitation of the heart if I. was flurried, or when I went upstairs. I was also short of wind, there was a dazzling before my eyes, and my sight was poor. "I used Doan's pills, which have proved splendid, better than anything I ever used. They acted well upon , my kidneys, and then ease in the other symptoms followed; I have been very well since. I did a hard day's work the other day, and was surprised at. not , feelinf, any ill effects, which proved to me how much good feeling pills bad done me. (Signed) M. A. Chadwick." • Doan's Backache Kidney Pills are two shillings and ninepence per box (six boxes for thirteen shillings and ninepence.) STRANGE WEDDING.

BY CHRISTIAN LYS,

fiuthor of "The Fortress of Yadasara," "The Mystery of Ladyplace," "The Flep,sworth Millions," "The Doctor's Idol," "The Opening Door," "Mr. Quixley of the Gate House," &c.

In the tragedies and comedies of ;ife much depends upon the setting. How little, compara- tively speaking, one thinks of a crime committed in an alley of East-End London! It seems natural Vint tragedies should occur there. But let the orlme happen in fashion's centre and everyone w 11 talk it into a great sensation. Here was a tragedy, not of the vulgar sort—of cowardly blows, or the taking of human life—but a very real tragedy nevertheless. Broken hearts are matter for amusement to those who do not understand ; science has no panacea for the mAlady, and the whole-hearted laugh it to scorn. Yet the malady is common enough, only its sAting is so often mean that it is overlooked.. Here it was different. The setting was all that w_nilth could devise. A thick carpet deadened t) silence the falling footstep. Costly furniture and bric-a-hrac were in the room, and pictures only possible to great wealth hung upon the wolls. The subdued light from a shaded lamp fell upon the woman's silk gown, and drew cilours from the diamonds upon her fingers. It ,st a glow, too; upon her beautiful Face—the t agedy in this setting of wealth and luxury. She had been playing. The piano stood open, and somet music had fallen on to the carpet. Now she idly turned the leaves of a new novel, bit she was not reading. She was thinking, d ifting backwards as she so constantly did when sic was alone, forgetting the present to touch w th memory's sensitive fingers a past which had o ice been happy. It was the memory of that t me which had been her strength during the five Img years of her married life. Yet, as often as sic thought so, she took herself to task for the Viought. Had she not her boy? Did she not c instantly steal up to his room in the evening a id draw the curtains back from his little bed to

s .tisfy herself that life still held something

p •ecio-us ? As she turned the !eaves listlessly there was a s mnd in the hall. With an effort she conjured _ _

iti? a smile into her sad face. It was a thin vmeer to the tragedy, but it had served success- filly for five years. As the door opened she half rose. "You are earlier than---" But it was not her husband. A servant crossed Vie room and handed her a card .upon a silver s aver. The colour came into her face as she r3ad the name. For a moment she hesitated, glancing towards the clock, and then with a little c ttch in her breath she said: "Shew him in.,, She rose from her chair and stood looking ab hr visitor in silence for some moments after the d)or had been closed behind him. "Robert ! " "I have come back as I promised, Ethel. Had I known how I should find you I should have s%ayed away. Do manY women break their oaths a: you have broken yours ? " "I had no alternative," she answered. • sit down? " The man made a little gesture of impatience as h complied. "I suppose not," and he looked quickly round Cie room. "Money commands; it does not ask. E;en now I could give you nothing like this, but i the old days you used to say you would be s ..tisfied with a little." "I do not understand why you remind me of flat," she said. "Forgetfulness has come too, has it? What e tnnot money do? Still, you did say so, and I, plor fool, believed you." "I spoke truly." "Then why are you George Fielden's wife ? " She did not reply immediately. She had &len asked herself the same question, and it'though the answer to it came easily enot4,ll a h ating doubt sometimes followed. Was she quite justified in making the sacrifice she had ? "I told you all in my last letter," she said after a pause. "You told me- ? " and then he broke off. °That letter never reached m 2." "Never reached you ? 7 "Never." "Then 3i)-ti 'know nothing "anly that I have been working for seven yetirg, toiling hard and living in miserly fashion, in order that I might come back to you and say: _ _ _ _ _

" Won't

It is possible that George Fielden would have altered his will when he had had time to think over it and to become more reasonable; but the next morning he was in a high fever and delirious. For three days he' continued in this state, and then sank into a stupor. Dr. Vacher, whom he had lately consulted, was called in, and he said that the patient's condition had probably arisen from some shock or undue excitement, and, considering the state of Mr. Fielden's heart and the general feebleness of his constitution, he could give little hope of his recovery. "There is brain trouble as well as heart, Mrs. Fielden."

The next day George Fielden died. The night before he was taken ill he had shewn his wife a sealed letter, and had placed it in a certain drawer in his desk.

"When anything happens to me you must open this letter. It will tell you where my will is, and explain certain things which I wish carried out with regard to my funeral."

Circumstances were against George Fielden. Not naturally suspicious, from the moment he found Robert -Hume in his drawing-room he began to watch his wife. He listened for a hidden meaning in every word she uttered, found some ground for speculation in her every action, and all the time he hated himself for doing so. He had known when he had married her that she did not love him ; she had been perfectly frank with him, and he had been content to marry her. Now the true regard and loyalty which she gave him seemed of little worth again4t the one thing lacking. He had never really given up the hope of Winning her love until now. Robert Hume's return had shown him that his wife's heart was a kingdom he could never hope to enter. In the bitterness of his ,soul he sometimes spoke angrily, bringing a sudden flush to his wife's cheek ; but she would have pitied him had she understood how many of the things he was tempted to say were choked back and remained, unspoken, how he fought as daily battle with him- self, ever loving her, more deeply, ever growing more jealous. The horrible wish to make her suffer as he did was alwaya in his mind. "Dion, Mr. • Hume, we will make all arrange- ments for to-morrow night," said Mr. Barton. "I will write to this solicitor and ask him to be present as a witness that the will has been duly complied with. I congratulate you on your decision; I believe it is the right one to make."

That was a strange wedding which made Robert Hume and Ethel Fielden man and wife. Outside was the midnight rumble of the streets, the world of pleasure and fashion going its several ways with light or heavy heart as it happened. Within the light was dim and cold, making the faces of the living almost as ashen as that of the man lying in his coffin placed on trestles at the foot of the bed. The clergyman, an old friend of Hume's, read the service in a subdued tone, and the answers were given in a low voice. And then it was over. Husband and wife stood hand-in-hand beside the coffin for a moment, and Ethel bent down and kissed the dead man's forehead. It was her forgiveness for what he had made her pass through. The rumble in ,the streets grew less and less until it was silent altogether. The lights in the room were out and the door was locked. A breath of night air stirred the curta'ns at the window a little, and a ray from a street lamp cast a yellow patch upon the ceiling just above the coffin. Without it was still, very still; even fashion and pleasure had gone home to rest. Within it was still, death still, but the wind was rising a little surely. There was a long sigh just now which sounded like the first breath of a coming storm, yet the curtains stirred n-lo more than they had done. There the sigh again, louder, deeper! it awakened sounds in the dead room. That' was a mouse in the skirting surely, and that—Great Heavens! the room was not death-still. It was full of life, struggling, pulsating, throbbing life. The trestles creaked, and fingers, with the muscles in them strained, grasped the sides of the coffin. Then the.whito figure rose to a sitting posture. The wild eyes glanced round the room and then at the strange and narrow bed. The man sighed again. For a moment he felt relieved, was glad that his horrible dream was not true, but the next-- "I (tin dead," he whispered, for his eyes - caught the shape of the coffin lid standing ready there and the glimmer of the brass plate upon it. "I am dead, dead to the world. It was no dream, but the truth. The, were married itt:-.4t now, here close b.!side me; She did bend to kiss me. ===== 0002601_19071109_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT

COUNCIL.

The Pollution of Sankey Brook.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday, Dr. Murray presiding. There were also present :—Messrs. R. Forshaw, W. Furness, A. Brooks, W. Houghton, J. Backhouse, W. Stanier, P. Longton, J. C. Sutton (clerk), F. Kerfoot (inspector of nuisances), Dr. Sephton (medical officer), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor), H. H. Burnage (assistant surveyor).

===== 0002601_19071109_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FUMES OF WIDNES.

The CLERK reported that further correspondence had passed between Messrs. Gibbon and Arkle, Liverpool, and himself with reference to the appointment of an inspector of alkali works within the Widnes and Run- corn area. Some time ago the authorities interested in the matter agreed to the appointment of an inspector and it was understood that the Warrington District, Council should pay £l7 2s. 31. per year. It had, how- ever, transpired that the respective proportions were being so alloted that Warrington's contribution would be £l7 2s. 3d. per half year. There was a proposal on foot now that an assistant inspector should be appointed. The whole matter would be discussed at a conference of the interested authorities to he held on November 21st at Liverpool. Two-thirds of the inspector's salary was payable by the local authorities and one-third by the Local Government Board. The salary of the assis- tant inspector would be paid in equal shares by the Local Government Board and the local authorities.

Mr. HouGuToN : What advantage is going to accrue to us by having an additional inspector? The CLERK said that personally he could not sea that it would be of any great value to the district but there 'were people who knew better than he on that point. In answer to Mr. Brooks, the SURVEYOR said the salary and expenses of the inspector (without ,the assistant) were expected to amount to £712 per annum. Mr. SACKROUSE said there was no doobt that inspec- tion of the works was beneficial to Cuerdley, Penketh and Sankey. Since the inspection was discontinued the works had turned out more acids than previously and this affected the farmers and, he assumed, the public health.

Mr. BROOKS asked if trade had been better r.t Widnes of late.

Mr. BACKHOUSE said he telieved the works had been busier. lie did not see why they should emit such fumes to the detriment of the people who had to live in the locality, without some check being kept on them. Mr. HOUGHTON: Are the duties of the inspector confined to alkali works? There are other chemical works in the district.

The INSPECTOR: He has to inspect all works. Mr. FORSHAW : Does Mr. Backhouse mean that agriculture is suffering?

Mr. BACKHOUSE : Yes.

The CLERK said it appeared to him that the Runcorn parishes would be more concerned than those in the Warrington district. Mr. 13AcKHousm agreed, as the winds often carried the fumes in the Runcorn direction.

The CLERK said be had known injury to be done even in the Whitley district by the fumes from Widnes. He hardly thought it we fair that Warrington and Run- corn should contribute in equal proportions when the Runcorn district was full of agricultural parishes. It was decided that the Chairman, Mr. Broas and the Surveyor should attend the conference at Liverpool and be empowered to agree to any arrangement pro- vided that it did not commit the Council to an expendi- ture of more than £25-per annum.

HEALTH OF _ THE DISTRICT.

Dr. SEPIITON reported that during the month there had been 29 births and 36 deaths. The death-rate was equal to 35 per 1,000 against 17.6 the average for the past three months and 23 for the correeponding month of last year. Twenty-five deaths occurrvl in the County Asylum, Winwick, so that the death-rate proper for the district was 10.7. The following cases of infectious diseases had been notified :—Scarlet fever, two cases ; diptheria, onb case. An epidemic of whooping cough had occurred at Burtonwood, thirty children being absent from St. Paul's school through that cause.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
OR FOB D BROOK.

Dr. SEPIITON informed the Council that he, along with the Surveyor and the Clerk, xi- et Dr. Coote Hibbert, medical officer to the Warrington Corporation, and visited Orford Brook. He understood from Dr. Hibbert that the Corporation said the condition of the brook must be remedied, but how the doctor did not 5a3. It was pointed out that the reason why the brook was so contaminated was mainly the fault of the Warring- ton Corporation in not carrying out the arrangements that had been made some years ago with regard to receiving sewage. Since the interview he had not heard an} thing further from Dr. Hibbert about the matter.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
ANOTIIER COMPLAINT OF POLLUTION.

The INSPECTOR read a letter from 'Messrs. J. and W. N. Hutchings, Howley Tannery, Warrington, dated November 2nd, in which they drew attention to the alleged serious pollution of Cockshot Brook. which had been going on for some time in the neighbourhood of Pedgate, but which had recently become so much worse that their water supply was threatened. Both at the Union Tannery and at their own the water was relied upon in their manufacturing processes, in fact it was on account of the water supply that the tanneries were originally bronght to Howley. They (the Council) would therefore see that this brook should be preserved from pollution, and to attain that end they had previously had to uphold their rights on two occasions. They hoped the matter would be brought befora the Council at their next meeting, and that the Council would at once recognize the urgency of the case and take steps to insure the immediate abatement of a very sf rious nuisance.

Mr. HOUGHTON thought the firm ought first to have approached the Wairington Corporation, inasmuch as the part ef the brook which they complained about was in the borough. _

SYMPATHETIC INSANITY IN TWINS.

A strange sympathetic bond existing be- tween twin sisters is related by Dr. G. S. Walker. From childhood there had been an unusual mutual attachment, and physically this bond was also strangely shewn by the fact that if one of them became ill the other was almcst sure to • manifest the same symp- toms. The culmination of this strange -rela- tionship came early in 1900. While away from home one of the sisters became violently in- sane, there being no apparent cause, no here- ditary predisposition, and no premonitory symptoms. After about a week the other sis- ter was sent for, and upon seeing her sister was attacked in the same way with acute mania. Both were removed to an asylum, and, as a precautionary measure, were kept in different wards, and no intercourse be- tween them permitted. The progress of the two eases was almost exactly identical. When one would have an acute attack, the other would be similarly affected. When one com- menced to improve, the other one also was better, and relapses occurred in both at prac- tically the same time.

A BACHELOR ON MARRIAGE.

Fashion seems really to extend its sway over even so solemn and serious a business as marrying and giving - in marriage, writes " Algernon " in the Gentlewoman. , The age at which people think of going in "double har- ness" is, for instance, perfectly different to what it was some years ago; also the relative ages of what the penny-a-liner calls "the con- tracting parties." The old Early and Mid-Vic- torian idea was that the young man of three or four-and-twenty ought to marry the young lady of eighteen, and that if a woman had not changed her estate by the time she was five- and-twenty it was safe to predict that she would live and die an old maid. Nowadays, brides whose hair is liberally sprinkled with grey are led to the hymeneal altar •in white satin and orange - blossoms as if they were eighteen, while an interesting •announcement made just the other day shews that even at five-and-forty it is quite possible to make - a bril- liant match. As to the relative ages of bride and bridegroom, May and December being wedded is the commonest thing in the world, and very often it is the bridegroom that is re- presented by May, the bride by December. Strangest of all, that the greater the disparity. in years the more successfully does the alliance appear to turn out, and as often as not the marriage of a widow to a man much younger than herself proves far more congenial than the typical young man and maiden "fusion of souls."

CLOTH COSTUME.

For the costume shewn in the sketch brown- and-black-striped cloth is used. The skirt is plain, and touches the ground all round. The coat, which is three-quarter length, is cut away at the corners, and is bound at the edges with brown silk braid. It is cut low at the neck, and finished with lopg revers faced with brown

, velvet, and fastened with three large ornamental buttons. The low-cut opening is partly filled in with a little fancy waistcoat of cream satin, trimmed with brown, black, and gold braid. The sleeves are the usual coat Shape, and are finished with a row of braid at the cuff. The mushroom-shaped hat is of fine black felt, with 'a broad white border, and is trimmed with a hand- some Nattier-blue feather mount. TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

MOCK-TURTLE SOUP.—Half a calf's head, take out the brains, and put as much water as you ivish to make soup, with sweet herbs, parsley, a few onions, and some lemon peel. Stew it gently until- the meat is tender; then take it out and let it get cold; thicken the soup with a little flour and butter, and strain it through a sieve. Add sherry, a little walnut and mush- room ketchup, pounded mace, and cayenne. Take the brains and beat them up with a little flour and two eggs into a light batter, 4ddinc; pepper and salt, and some parsley chopped very fine; then take the, yolks of two eggs and hea.c them up; rptika them into as many, portions as you please, tying each in a bit of muslin, and bail them in little round balls; the batter made with the brains is to be fried into cakes a light brown. When the head is cold it must be cut into small pieces and laid in the gravy, the ealceS ana egg, witii ..,ome forcemeat Dail*, to be laid on the top of the soup just before it is sent to table. Every particle of fat must be removed from the surface before warming the for t-S9 table.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

NOTABLE METEORITES.

To the student of meteoric iron great ad- vantages are offered by Vienna, for its museum . contains the oldest of the large col- lections •of meteorites in the world. This gathering of 100 years has representatives of 615 meteoric falls in different localities. It contains 2,075 specimens, weighing in all three- and a-half tons: Some of tile specimens ere of historic interest.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
SUBMARINE TELEGRAPHY.

According to Mr. Charles Bright there are some 257,000 miles of cable in all at the bot- tom of the sea, representing over £52,000,000 sterling, each line costing about -£2OO per mile to inake and lay. The average useful life of a cable nowadays may be anything between thirty and forty years, according to circum- stances. About 6,000,000 messages , are con- veyed by the world's cable throughout the year, or .15,000 a day, the working speed for any one cable being up to 100 words per minute under present conditions. About 90 per cent. of -these messages are sent in word or cipher code, for business and official pur- poses.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
PRINTING RAINBOW EFFECTS.

A patent has been granted in Germany for a machine which will print different colours 'on a fabric in such a way that they shade one into another. If, then, the proper sequence of colour is observed, says, the Dyer and Calico Printer, a rainbow-like effect is pro- duced: The different dyes are brought to the machine by means of ' endless bands, which are directed to various parts of the printing cylinder, according to the .effect desired. An alternative method is to make the printing cylinder take up different colours along its 'length by dividing the supply trough into com-. partments, each containing a different colour.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VOICE AND THE HAND.

In. a study, which purports to be entirely scientific, of the alleged connection between the physical and mental Character of an indi- vidual and his .handwriting, Mons. Solange Pellat, an expert attached to the Tribunal of the Seine, Paris, maintains that distinct rela- tions exist between the handwriting and the voice. An expert, he declares, can determine from the handwriting whether the writer's voice is high or low in pitch, sonorous or veiled, harsh, or soft and agreeable. But he remarks that in all cases where it is sought to determine character from handwriting, great pains should be taken to choose for examina- tion only writing that has been done under normal conditions.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
PORCELAIN AND ELECTRICITY.

- -------- Among the industries that have been pro- foundly modified by the advent of electricity into daily use is that of making porcelain. Formerly artistic considerations alone governed the various Operations of the work- men in porcelain, but now, since this sub- stance is employ,ed for insulators in all elec- tric installations, scientific processes have been introduced in its manufacture alich de- mand a great deal of special attention. The exact amount of contraction that the clay un- dergoes, the ,exact temperature to which it is submitted in the process of baking, the Con- stant employment of instruments for measur- ing the temperature and for determining the size of certain pieces—such are among the .essentials in the modern art of porcelain. making for electric purposes.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
GLASSMAKING BY LIGHTNING.

-- Tubes of glass made by lightning are often found in sand. The electricity passes into the ground and melts the silicious material, form- ing little. pipes the inside diameter of which' represents the bore of the "thunderbolt," Such tubes measuring as much as 27ft. in length have been discovered. No-doubt exists As to the method of their manufacture, inas. much as people have sought for them and ,iug them up still hot from places freshly struck by lightning. Attempts have been made to re- produce them artificially by passing a power- ful current of electricity through finely-pow- dcred glass. In this way pipes nearly an inch long and about the size of a darning needle have been obtained. From the omparative size one gets a notion of the enormous energy of lightning.

ORIGIN OF FINS,

A controversy has been long. in existence with ,regard to the origin of the paired fins of fishes, and thus the limbs of all vertebrates, for it is admitted that the. limbs of all animals are derived originally from the primitive fin. PLANT AND ANIMAL SYMBIOSIS.

The presence or absence of chlorophyll, the green colouring matter of plants, was at one time suggested as a distinguishing mark be- tween the vegetable and aninial kingdoms. The common .green hydra of our ponds, which was known to owe its colour to chlorophyll, was quoted as an exception. It -was after- wards found, however, that the green colour was really due to a species of one-oelLed alga, living in symbiosis with the hydra. A- green amoeba, which also owes its colour to a species of alga, is likewise known. Still more interesting, perhaps, says the Globe, is the case of, the green worm investigated by Messrs. Keeble and Gamble, and described by them in the Quarterly .Journal of the Microscopical Society. Like the hydra and amoeba, this worm, a species of eonvoluta, owes its colour to a green unicellular alga. When first hatched' the worm is colourless, and contains neither green cells nor germs of the same. If, however, the worm, or egg, be placed in sea-water, the infecting 'organism settles on it, and the worm becomes green.

The green worm can also be; so i 0 speak, BUILT UP FROM THE COLOURLEWVORT+I

and the green alga. If the egg be kept free from infection a colourless worm can be batched and reared from it. The green alga can also be isolated and cultivated. The authors have added the green alga to the colourless worm, and thus produced. the green convolute. What iS the nature of the associa- tion between the animal and the plant in this case? In the first stage it is a case of symbi- osis, an association for mutual advantage. As the animal develops the symbiosis passes into a. parasitism of the worm on the algal cells. These are found to act as an excretory system to the animal, while the latter is observed to cease from the ingestion of food. And it has been snewn that Lee green cells taken t-om the adult wcrni are incapable of sepa- rate existence. This is due to degeneration of the aucleuu.

SCIENTIFIC PLANT FORCING.

The best natural conditions for plant growth are artificially supplied in, one of the greenhouses of the Royal Botanic Gardens. On cloudy days an artificial sun, in the form of a water-surrounded arc-lamp automati- cally moved along a tramway, sends light in the natural way to every part • of) the plants. Earthenware pipes from the engine diffuse a carefully - regulated supply of moist air charged with carbonic acid, iron. pines distri- bute heat to 'keep up the best temperature, and an- -electrostatic machine stimulates • the soil to convert insoluble into soluble nitrates, and arouses the roots to the highest degree of activity.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
SCOTTISH PEAT MOSSES.

An investigation in Scottish peat mosses by Mr. Francis J. Lewis elicits the fact that in the southern uplands the stratified plant remains shew a gradual change from a Wood- land condition to that of heath and MOSS, and then back again to woodland. In some locali- ties a bed of Arctic plants is interposed be- tween the two woodland ages. A correspond- ing sequence of the beds in widely separated areas is Considered as furnishing evidence of varying climatic differences during the deca- dence,. of the Glacial period. Arctic plants are 'everywhere absent from the base of the peat, wl)ich consists of a forest -bed contain- ing birch and hazel remains.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
RESPIRATION OF SEEDS.

Experiments on the respiration of seeds in a dry or resting condition have been de- scribed by P. Becquerel in Comptes Rendus. Generally speaking, seeds in the dry state, when kept in the dark, give off a trace of carbonic gas, and absorb a small. amount -*of oxygen. This gaseous exchange is greater when the seeds are exposed to the light, owing to its power of favouring oxidation. The testa of the seed, is an important factor in determining the rate of respiration. In Ricinus the gaseous exchange was higher in the testa alone than in the seeds from which they had been removed. Dehydration also plays an important part, and An some in- stances no gaseous exchange took place in the absence of moisture.

Grappenhall came to the front in a way she had never done before, and was well backed up by all the divisions of Cheshire, and, further than that, by all the divisions throrghout the country with very few exceptions. They felt that their time had come in returning such a magnifcent Liberal majority to Parliament, and Mr. King had now come to give some account of his stewardship. The great wish he (Mr. Long) had in regard to these questions was that men should hear both sides of. He did not say for a moment that Liberals were always undeniably right in their policies but they tried to do the best they could for the county. LADY BkRLOW AND SOCIALISM.

• The Hon. Lady Barlow said that when she cdme up to the north coontry she was so appalled at the oonditions In some of the big towns that she began to trouble her- self as to what she could do to improve matters. She came to the conclusion that if she had fifty times the fortune she had, she could inake but very little differ- ence to her poorer neighbours and that the only way to remedy matters was by legislatiou, That was how the

came to he a politician. As far as she could see, one of the fint quegrions to be dealt with was education for the children. She did not mean simply book learning, but education in a large sense which would include care for the physical wants of the children. That might sound somewhat advanced to some of them, but it was only what had been done abroad for years past. They should never gr Klee the money spent in educating or caring for the children, because that was one of the chief defences of a nation. Mothers should bring all the force they could to hear on the better education of girls, became what we wanted every girl in the country to know, whether she be the daughter of duke or of a crossing sweeper, was how to care for her home and family, and that had not been taught in the schools as it should have been. She was happy to say that improvements were taking place and in Cheshire they were far in advance of many other counties, but much remained to be done. As for the trouble that was being made about Socialism, she did not think they need worry their heads in the least about it. Mercifully, the nation was moving and people were beginning to think. They did not all think on the same lines, but. what did that matter? There would not be that immense weight of the people who did not care and who said : "It always has been so and always must be." There were people now who said: `` ft shall not be so." There always had been an advanced opinion and they must not be afraid of it. The Tory party were making the most of the matter. They had found that Protection, Home Rule and other herrings they had tried to draw across the track did not work, and Socialism was their latest. One Bill they muzit be prepared for next Session, continued Lady Barlow, was the Licensing Bill. What form it would takeshe did not know, but if it were in the form oflocal option she would be thankfnl because it was a democratic principle that a district should decide for itself what it wanted. He did not govern Ireland firmly,and outrage and disorder ea ra worse there than they had been for years,. but in India he promptly stopped sedition, which, was the- proper course to take. To be consistent, however, be ought to have deported Mr. Keit. Hardie who had preached sedition right and left. Speaking at Warrineton. Mr. Sydney Buxton, M.P., complained that the House of Lords allowed the Trades Disputes Bill to pass. If it were a bad and wicked Bill why did the Government pass it ? He supposed it was in the hope that the House of Lords would throw it out, and Liberals would then make capital of it. Lord Lansdowne, however; was too cute, for though he condemned the Bill, and said it was wicked, he said " We sill let it pass." (Laughter.) Mr. King. in reply, said Indio,. and Ireland were two totally different populations existing under quite different conditions. The- Government regarded the Irish people as being on the same plane of civilization as ourselves, but in India, there mare 130 different tribes of di ffarent nationalities and religious faiths. He had stiracient confidence in Mr. Morley as a Radical administrator and, lover of freedom and haa:anity, to believe that he would act in what he thought were the best' interest for India. (Applause.) As to Mr. Buxton's speech, he (Mr. King) thought it ' must have been somewhat misrepresetted by Mr. Baxter. He had no doubt Mr. Buxton was illustrating the position of the House of Lords by showing that • although they rejected many measures which the country really wanted and which the Liberals had passed, they passed the Trades Disputes- Bill although their. ex-Lord Chancellor and their leader denounced it as iniquitous land unjust. It vat-, fer the Lords to explain why they I passed it. Lord Lansdowne said they were going to I fight the Commons and the peopte and they must choose theirown ground. They dare not fight on the Trades ! Disputes Bill so they passed it. (Applause.) A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the meetang

===== 0002601_19071109_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER.

BOVHUBIin 9th, 1907,

The chief result of the THE railway struggle will be a SETTLEMENT, great triumph for Mr. LLOYD GEORGE. Some savage tribe in South Africa conferred on Mr. CHAMBERLAIN the title of " The man who sees things through.', This was not exactly descriptive of Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN'S character, but it applies very well to his old opponent Mr. LLOYD GEORGE. He is at this moment the man who is seeing thing i through. No President of the Board of Trade ever had so difficult a matter to deal with. So far as that goes, indeed, no President of the Beard of Trade ever attempted to deal with such a situation. This in itself is remarkable. The exist- ing Government is not in power for the purpose of receiving salaries, but with the view of remedying public grievances. It is a Government which has frankly accepted the idea that it holds office for the purpdse of being useful. Mr. LLOYD GEORGE stands in a particular way for the exemplificatioa of this idea. He set himself from the first to prevent such a conflict between capital and labour as would produce consequences hitherto inexperienced, and nobody who knew him well ever doubted that he would succeed.

The questions that have been raised by the railway dispute will, in our opinion, never again subside. It would have been better, if one may use Scriptural language, to have dealt with the enemy in thegate whilst they were in the way with him. So far as the interests of the railway share- holders are concerned, it was not advisable that there should be an exposure of the conditions of life on our railways. The national cons...;ieuce has been shocked by what has come to light as to the rate of wages to be earned and the number of hours that must be worked in order to earn the wages. What has become matter of public knowledge in these respects can never be forgotten. It must influence all future legislation in regard to rail- ways. No railway Bill can now slip through the Houses of Parliament in the old, quiet way. It will be scanned from an entirely new point of view.

There is no reason whatever why the organisa- tions formed by the staffs of our railways should not live on good terms with the directors. It is all a matter of temper, or to speak mole accur- ately, perhaps, of temperament. The directors will excuse us, we hope, if we say first of all that they are not quite the wisest business men in the world, and, than, that in order to behave as wise men and business men, they should follow the course that men quite as important as themselves have been compelled to pursue, who have in the long run come to the conclusion that this com_ pulsion was to their advantage. It is not good for the nation, and it is not good for either party,that two great interests such as the railways and those who are employed on the railways should be in conflict. The country cannot afford it, and, if a strike were to be continued over a few days, the country would not permit it to proceed further. To have a Government and a Minister capable of dealing with such a situition and determined to "see the thing through," is a new experience in political and industrial life.

The first Town Council I To-DAY's ELECTION meeting for the municipal OF MAYOR AND year 1907-8 takes place at ! ALDERMEN. 12 o'clock noon to-day for the election of Mayor and Aldermen in place of the gentlemen whose term of , office has expired. Alderman Smethurst will, of . course, receive the support of all parties in his re-appointment as Mayor, the details having• already been cut and dried. In all probability the! election of Aldermen will also take a usual course, I the rumours of party differences notwithstanding. The retiring Ahlermen are Messrs. Smethurst, Bolton, Evans, Forshaw and Francomb, but the last-named does not seek re-election and will to-day cease to be a member of the Council after service extending over 26 years. Messrs. Smethurst, Bolton, Evans and Forshaw will probably be re-elected,and as it is reported that the two political parties have agreed upon recog- nising seniority once more, the likelihood is that Councillor Luke Ellison will be appointed to the vacant position. That is the state of affairs at the time of writing, but as our Conservative friends are to have a private meeting at 11 a.m. to-dayllthere may be eleventh hour developments. According to rumour—and very circumstantial rumour—there is a further Aldermanic vacancy in addition to the one caused by the resignation of Alderman Francomb. We are informed that Alder man James Fairclough has also resigned on account of ill-health, and that his written resigna- tion accompanied by the usual fine of is., is in possession of His Worship the Mayor, and more- over, has been in his possession for more than a week at least. We hope some enterprising and public-spirited councillor will ask the Mayor in open Council whether this is a fact,because ruinoar —perhaps a lying jade after all—suggests that this resignation has been kept back for party political reasons. It is certainly quite obvious that if the vacancy is not declared until after to-day's election of Alderman the newly appointed gentlemen will be in a position to record their votes in favour of the person whom they think best fitted to succeed Alderman Fairclough. And all but one of these new Aldermen will, according to every indication, be Conservatives. There is a significance about these facts—if facts they are—which it is impoE- sible to overlook.

PReCEDENCE.

The question of precedence is usually a vexed one wherever it arises, and in the Warrington Town Council it is more than ordinarily provocative of dissension. Our readers will recollect -several variations of the order of preference" determining the seniority of members of the Council." A few years ago there was a very unjust and unwise regulation which, if a member ceased to hold office for more than three mouths, all his past service counted for nought when he was re-elected. Owing to this rule, the history of which does not bear close examination, Alderman Pierpoint was kept out of the Aldermanic circle while much younger members in point of actual service were promoted " over his head." Mr. Pier- point lost his seat at one of the November elections, and when he finally won his way back to the Cotmcil he found this rule in operation and thus lost many years of service prior to tlie break in continuity. Subsequently the Stand 'mg Order was altera3 and the words "continuous and uninterrupted " omitted in reference to a member's tenure of office. Precedence has thus been determined of late date by length of service pure and simple. But at a private meeting of the Council on Mon- day a resolution was proposed and carried which will re-establish the objectionable clause insisting on continuity of service, if it is confirmed at the meeting to-day. We sincerely :hope it will be defeated, and that the Council will not stultify itself in this inexcusable fashion. It looks as if some members are prepared to blow hot and blow cold according to the Individual affected by the seniority rule. If a man has served so many years as a Councillor, and then been absent for three months or even three years he is entitled to the credit and value of the service when he re-enters the Council. That is the only sensible and just view of the matter, and we hope it will be upheld to-day.

Owing to the result of the THE WATCH recent elections special CC3IMITTEE. interest is taken in 63 formation of the Watch Committee. Last year there was only one mein- ber of " the trade '• on this Committee, viz., Alderman Bolton, and we note that he is again on the list to be submitted for confirma- tion to-day. The other nine gentlemen provisionally appointed are Aldermen_ Pierpoint, Smethurst and Tinnion,Councillors Burton,Ellison, Evans, Dr. Joseph, SAnkey and Wilkinson. There has always been an objection to Alderman Bolton being on this Committee because he is a brewer,and the same objection not only holds good to-day but it wa3 voiced at the caucus meeting on Monday. We say without hesitation that no member who is directly interested in the scde of drink ought to have a seat on this Committee, which, of course manages the police force of the borough. It is a matter for some congratulation that there are no other interested parties on this Committee, and we think it would certainly be to Alderman Bolton's credit if he resigned a position which may not only be a delicate one in the event of any licensing prosecution, but which is at all times i condemnatory.

Since November Ist, there 'THE TRADE" is no doubt that the thinking AND THE CourTen. people in Warrington have felt disappointed and dis• gusted that the only candidates returned to the Town Council after a contest are members of "the trade." The result of the elections here stamps Warrington as a drink-ridden community, and we hope the forces of sobriety and reform in the town will at once bestir themselves in order to prevent the spread of an evil tendency on the part of the electorate. We believe if half a dozen publicans had been in the field they would all have been returned—the workingman elector seems so enamoured of this kind of candidate. The results at Newcastle have a peculiar interest for Warrington at the present time, for the simple reason that every one of the new councillors in this enlightened city is in favour of Temperance reform, while three nominees of the liquor trade have been displaced. This surprising and gratifying result has not been obtained by chance. In Newcastle there is a "United Temperance Electoral Council " formed a little over three years ago for the avowed ohj-32t of opposing the election of liquor sellers to the City Council and of returning representatives " who will consider the welfare of the people rather than the selfish interests of a trade . • , which not only demoralises the people, but hinders and blasts the progress of the Gospel." The Electoral Council is composed of representative members of the churches—both Episcopal and Nonconformist—also representatives from all the Temperance societies (save one, the C.E T.S.) Individual members comprise Liberals, Cm- servatives, Unionists, It idicals, Socialists, etc:, etc. The Electoral Council, therefore, knows no political party, but it is a power in the city. Systematic opposition has been raised against all the "trade candidate 3 by circular, canvass and public meeting, and the result is a feather in the cap of the Electoral Council. What is possible in Newcastle, Should be possible in Warrington. We badly want an Electoral Council here. The Temperance party as a party is supine and impotent,while the churches either Episcopal or Nonconformist, never unite in a practical way to fight the drink evil. More might certainly be expected from Free Churchmen, and we think that they, and the avowed temperance adherents, will be lacking in a true conception of Cl,ristianify if they do not take up the challenge of the trade in War- rington. Can a Temperance Electoral Council not be formed in the once "good old town ?" Who will help? We shall be glad to receive any communication on the subject from readers who possess a real enthusiasm for clean and honest municipal Government!

The forthcoming season's THE MUSICAL work of the WarringtOn SOCIETY. Musical Society promises to be of unusual interest and involves, at the outset, an effort at once so ambi- tious and praiseworthy that it is fervently to be hoped that public appreciation of its consumma- tion in the first concert will be shown in a measur3 of support both commensurate and generous. It is as notorious as it is regrettable that the Musical Society has for years past thanklessly laboured in the service of the public in the giving of concerts, which, while distinctly elevating and educational in character, have been pretty well foredoomed to failure from a commercial point of view. But We have hopes that from seed thus patiently sown a harvest of awakened public interest may be reaped by the Society in the season just opening. For its first concert a performance is announced in a fortnight's time of Sir Edward Elgar's "Dream of Gerontius," an important modern work of such magnitude of conception and such com- plexity in construction that only the strongest and best equipped societies in largely populated centres can attempt its successful presentment. And here it is that we recognise, on the part of the Society's management and of all concerned in carrying out its projects, a supreme attempt at least to deserve a success which,under unfortunate present-day conditions, may not always be com- manded. That the thing will be well done the society's high reputation does not permit us to doubt, though an adequate performance of Elgar's great work involves both a very numerous and very capable body of vocalists as well as a large and elaborate orchestra. And to think that so monumental a piece of music can be listened to for an outlay of sixpence! TO the credit of Warringtonian musical enterprise we can ask in vain—where in the kingdom can 130 high a privilege be paralleled ? To all interested in hearing " The Dream of Gerontius heartily commend the careful perusal of some highly histructive notes on this work written by Canon Morley Stevenson and Mr. Frank a Crossley, to be found in another column. -- Liberalism in the Knutsford KNUTSFORD DIVISION division should receive a LIBERALISM. Stimulus as the result of the meetings which Me. A. J. King, M.P. is addressing in all parts of the big constituency. The indefatigable Member is able to present the electors with such a fine record of work accomplished, particularly in the agricultural interest, that they are being more than ever convinced that they took the right course in helping to return a progressive Govern- ment to power. The meeting held at Grappenhall on Monday evening was of special interest, for Mr. King was accompanied by S rJ. Emrnott Barlow) Bart., M.P., who unsuccessfully contested the division in 1885, and Lady Barlow. Happily a great change has come over the political situation since the days when Sir Emmott Barlow fought a sturdy, uphill battle, and Sir Ernmott was cordial in his congratulations to Mr. King 0.0 his distinction of being the first to wrest the constituency from Tory rule. All the speeches were forceful and well-reasoned, and emphasised the fact that the Government is maintaining the best traditions of Liberalism in its home and foreign policy. Notwithstanding the earnestness and zeal of the Government, and the fact that theY, have such a great majority,they are still hatoperen in their schemes of reform by the reactionary and biased hereditary Chamber. The electors of t.he Knutsford division will be gratified that Mr. King has unhesitatingly declared his intention of 8.1213- porting the Prime Minister in his determination to curtail the power of the Lords and ensure that the will of the peopla shall prevail. The meeting had the pleasure of hearing a delightful little speech by Lady Barlow who impressed all by her obvious sincerity as a social reformer and deep concern for the helpless and poverty-stricken.

o a After the vianis, nothing went down better at the .uYelilLg Club dinner than the President's crushing criticisms of the Warrington roads and streets. His suggestion that the Mayor and Corporation should lake a cycle ride from Warrington to Gra,ppenhall, Stretton and Walton was hailed with enthusiasm by all except the Mayor. No doubt the latter felt a trifle uncertain of his seat—on a cycle —and also , some !'esponsibility for the shocking pavements in the borough. They certainly are a disgrace, and moreover theY are becoming notorious for miles around. Mr. yaylor did not exaggerate when he sail there were holes two inches deep. There are plenty, and some of 'them a foot or more in length. It strikes us that nothing short of Mr. Naylor's remedy will move the Warrington Corporation to a fit sense of its short- -comings in the matter of strest paving. * The members of the Warrington Cycling Club are justly proud of the high position which their organiza- tion holds in the world of wheels. It has just attained its majority and was never in a more flourishing con- dition. Good fellowship and sociability are two of the great watchwords of the cyclists and these were the 'dominant characteristics of the successful gathering beld at the Patten Arms Hotel on Thursday evening to celebrate the 21st anniversary. Crosfield M.P. proposed the toast of The Cycling Club "in a speech couched in the lighter vein and embodying remote reminiscenes of his own adventures Oil the old-time " bone-shaker " and " spider." The, function could not have been more successful and the prospects of the club are full of promise. * *

• - 'Sa *nkey Brook has been a source of nuisance for 25 sears at least, and during that time its condition has not improved much notwithstanding the intermittent • efforts of various authorities. The Warrington Rural -Council returns to the attack oftenest, and perhaps it will some day achieve something. St. Helens is the principal contributor to the foulness of the stream Which, according to Dr. Sephton, comes into the glass town perfectly clear and leaves it like an ink-pot. Prom experience, we know that the colour of the " ink "varies frequently. The Rural Council and the Warrington Corportion are not quite free from complicity in the fouling of the brook, and until they 'are they cannot, of course, shake a very stern fist at St. Helens. A. deputation from the R.D.C, has been appointed this week to inquire once more into the Causes of pollution and the powers of the Council. Meanwhile —and perhaps for another 25 years—the brook will flow placidly and unpleasantly on. * The garden city movement has spread to Manchester. On Saturday afternoon the Lord Mayor of that city cut the first sod of an estate that is to be laid out in garden city form at Burnage by a company called the Man- chester Tenants Limited. The estate covers eleven acres, the only one hundred and thirty houses will be built 'The rents will vary from ss. 6d. to Os. and the houses Will be tenanted by people capable of appreciating the benefits conferred by semi-rustic environment. There is to be a social club, a recreation ground, allotments or lawns, and a bowling green, while a fountain will Play in one of the principal avenues. The Manchester Tenants Limited is a co-partnership society. Every -tenant will be invited and sexpected to become a member. The tenant will have a share of whatever profits may be made, but instead of che profits being paid out in cash they will be credited to the tenant as share capital, till it amounts to the value of the house in which he lives. Then he will be his -own landlofd.

* * * "The Fairhope Courier" of August 30th, has a -most instructive article on one small baby, the son of the son of William Waldorf Astor, who in due course of time will inherit .the Astor estate. It points out that at the present time Mr. Astor owns in New York .alone real estate worth, at present prices, abciut .-;£60,000,000 sterling; but what the land will be worth 'when the newly-born baby " comes to his own "-- say, in twenty-one years' time —nobody can even guess. In the meanwhile, however, America's favour- -able (?) balance of trade, of exports ovor imports, will 130 annually swollen by the rent, tribute, or interest ' accruing to the Astor grandfather, father, and grand- baby. If this =punt of wealth (so sorely needed by millions of the industrious citizens of New York) 'accrued to some idle mandarin of China, or some subtle attractive power wafted it to the denizens of .the planet of Mars, all would agree in denouncing -the situation. As it is, however, the force that sucks the wealth from its producers in New York, without any return save the permission to live and work on that favoured portion of the earth's surface, is popularly regarded as natural, and men, otherwise sane, delude themselves and others in the belief that —like poverty in the midst of plenty, of which it is the direct cause—it is one of the necessary accom- paniments of our civ:lisation. •

"Trial by Jury." A delightful musical evening was provided by the I members of Messrs. Crosfield's choir and orchestra on Thursday, and gave the greatest pleasure to the audience which filled the men's dining hall. These organisations have made very gratifying progress and the entertainment reflected great.credit upon all con- cerned and especially upon Mr. F. J. Lord, the con- ductor, who ljas taken the utmost Interest in the advancement of music at the works. The first portion of the programme was of a miscellaneous character , add included the overture "Bohemian girl," waltz "Rouge et Noir," Mazurka "La Gypsy," and the march "Stars and Stripes" which were given with precision and taste by the orchestra. Mr. S. Pyatt contributed a cornet solo and gr. J. Gilbert a pic,colo solo, each item being given with such ,great ability as to call forth warm applause. The vocal trio" Festina Lento" was very creditably interpreted by Misses C. Hastings and L. Hewitt and Mr. E. Walker and the Voices of Misses Hastings and Hewitt blended well in th- duet" 0, that we two" The selections by the Warrington Concertina Band" Hansel and Gretel " and " Songs of Wallace," 'under the conductorship of Mr. E. Smith, were an exceedingly popular feature. The second half Of the programme consisted of the Gilbert and Sullivan operetta " Trial by Jury " which the choir and orchestra have previously given with conspicuous success. The repetition proved a marked advance on the iaitial effort, all the performers sustaining their parts capably and fully realizing the rich humour of the work. Mr. A.. H. Crosfield, M.P., who had been speaking at the Cycling Club dinner during the earlier part of the evening, showed his appreciation of the work of the choir and and orchestra by attending for a short time. The entertainment will be repeqted this (Saturday) evening , and another crowded attendance is anticipated.

The Examiner, Saturday, November 9, 1907.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. RUGBY FOOTBALL. By 4ir—ais t."

The result of the match at Salford came as a surprise to Warrington people, who quite expected the Wirepullers to go down before the Reds. A drawn game was, therefore, a gratifying result and the gain of a useful point. I predicted last week that Warrington would lose if Lomas was p'aying, but the Salford crack was nursing an injury and this put the teams on an equality, for most of the Warrington stars were absent. The display given by the team reflects great credit on the reserves, who without exception distinguished themselves r daring some portion of the game.

There were several uncommon features about the. match. For one thing, I never remember Warrington scoring four goals and no tries. The feat was rendered all the more remarkable by the fact that in the two previous matches Warrington scored eight tries and only one goal out of twice as many attempts. MI Warrington's 8 points on Saturday were got before the interval, Thomas placing two penalties, and Tilley and Cartwright both dropping fine goals. It must be a long time since two goals were dropped by Warring- ton players in one match, certainly not since the days of J. Cross. The art of drop-kicking seems to have died out, with the single exception of Albert Gold- thorpe, who is still dropping and placing goals in the old sweet way. On Saturday he landed seven of 'em, —and his team might well be undefeated. A dropped goal for Warrington is a distinct rarity, and person- ally I do not like to see backs popping at goal too frequently. A man who can, however, do the needful when necessary is a valuable asset. But there is only one " Albert.'

This sequence of goals gave the Reds a shock, and ! up to the interval they could only scrape up a doubtful sort of try. With a five points lead in the second half Warrington ought to have won, for they had beaten the home team in the scrum and in combination all through. A. error of judgment was, I think, made by the Warringtou skipper, Lees, in bringing Thomas out of the pack. Hallam used to do the same thing with the idea of strengthening the defence, but the idea is a mistaken one unless the opposing backs are praying to) clever. On Saturday, the Warrington backs were more than holding their own, and the result of putting Thomas in the centre was that Sa ford began to get the ball and attack persistently. If the War- rington forwards had been kept intact the Warrington score might have been increased. Lees failed to realise that the best defence is attack. Salford eventu- ally got a try behind the goal after the ball had hit one of the posts, and Metson converting put the teams on an equality. Heath got hurt in the bargain,. and it took Warrington all their time to stave off defeat. There was no doubt they were the better side, and a try might have been gained if an Attacking policy had been persisted in.

Chief honours go to Tilley, who gave a superb display at full back and frequently baffled the Reds with his fine clearances, while he also captured John when the little half back looked like scoring. Dickenson and Cartwright both gave a good display in the centre —in fact the combination was far superior to anything on the home side. A little more speed on the wings would have made the issue certain. Kenyon and Dickenson both got away once only to be overhauled by Cook, the Devonport Albi n wing, who was 'about the speediest and best of the Salford threequarters. The Warrington for- wards again played a fine game, and young Boardman shone particularly in the 1005 e.,. de looks like upholding the family name as a scrummager. It is a pity that the reserve forwards are such a young lot on the whole. In this department the Club is rather badly off for talent, and a couple of new men of ex- perience and weight would be valuable acquisitions. The Northern League table up-to-date is as follows :

There was a fine game .between the " A" teams of Salford and Warrington at Wilderspool, and the home team did well to win by 15 points to 3 points for the Reds Juniors proved a smart lot. Their combination was capital, and if the final pass had not so often gone astray the result of the match might have been different. Warrington's play was rather ragged, but enlivened by several bursts of individual cleverness. Strong forward work and general determination Won the match. There is some good raw material in Daintith, Tinkler, and Harrison, but all want praotioe for a season or so in Combination football.

Warrington play their first trmtch in South Wales to-day, -when the Ebbw Vale team will be met and, I expect, vanquished. The team left Warrington at 5.50 last night and travelled to Hereford for the night, going on to Ebbw Vale at 9.20 this morning. The return journey takes a similar course, to-night being spent at Hereford again, and the players arriving home on Sunday morning. The trip will probably cost the club 40 or £7O.

A. strong team has been selected as follows : Tilley, full back; Taylor, Dickenson, Isherwoad and Kenyon, threequarters ; Brooks and Jenkins, half- backs ; Thomas, A. Boardman, P. Boardman, Shugars, Harmer, and Belton, forwards. The skipper, S. Lees, finds it difficult to get away, so Brooks and Jenkins will have their first experience as partners. They ought to make a strong combination.

A match with Liverpool City has been arranged for the "A" team, and the followfog have been chosen to represent the locals :—Hewitt, full back; Laudor, Lloyd, Jordan, and Forster, three-quarters; Daintith and Tinkler, h tlf backs ; Bradshaw, Holbrook, Jules, Preston, Fairhurst and Worley, forwards.

Tommy Hockenhull has this week become mine host of the " Rose and Crown," Bridge-street, and I wish him success in his new sphere. There is no prospect of him playing football again for some time, as he has yet to undergo an operation. Still, his ootball days are not considered over yet.
