THE VOICE OF LIIBOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. M.S.& must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

Will all those Liberals .who have a.sense of propor•

tion please take notice of the following condemnation of Socialism ? Speaking at a de.bite in connection with the Watrington Progressive Labour League last week Arr. Howes is reported t 3 have said : "He opposed S3cialisna because it would b 3 the greatest curse that ever came t ) the workingman, because it would kill the home life and destroy all domestic ties, and because it was anti-religious in the most thorough sense. It dealt only with creating a huge monopoly, and handing it over to the Government officials. The curse would be so great that the work- ingmen would protest against it and smash it down in less than twelve months."

The Tories have made some nasty, untrue state- ments but it has been left to the founder of a Pro-

gress..ve Labour League to more than equal them in - -

Vituperative ability. Last week-end the Tory newspapers came out with head lines the winner of the Grand National would have had some difficulty in negotiating, stating that Socialists had been "routed," "crushed," " wiped out," &c., &c., when, as a matter of fact, the average polls for the Socialist and Labaur candidates were much higher than in previous years and instead of being" wiped out " there has been a net gain of four seats. The movement is stronger t3-day than it was before the "- terrible onslaught" on the inuoceats

began. . Owing to the pressure on our allotted space my reply to Mr. OE. Parker is held over until next week. In the meantime, I would commend Mr. Parker's reply to "Perplexed "to my readers as it has con- siderable interest and bearing on the topic under our discussion, and shows a decided advance towards Socialism on anything which he his yet addressed to

_ this column. - There have been many speeches during the past week on "Socialism" go:ne of the speakers have adopted a reasonable attitude. A little more know- ledge and we shall have.no need to complain of" Free Lore" arguments: Hy impression is that if some of those who are condemning Socialism because of its alleged " Free‘ Love "ideas, really and truly believed that Socialism stood. for " Free Love" they would embrace it with both arms. It is the cleansing

system that they really object to. JOHN WORKNIAN. "THE' WHOLE: FREAT OF HIS LABOUR." Sir, —The letter written by one wh3 signs himself " Perplexed" is of considerable interest. Without any vagueness the writer takes a bee line to the important points of difficulty. I cannot agree with his opinion that to give every- one the whole fruit of his labour is impractical and

_ unjust. "Perplexed " points out by illustrations the difficulties of carrying this out in proof that it is impracticable, but he does not show wherein it is unjust. Surely it-is just that what a man produces by his brain or hand is his against all corners, for what other claimant can show-n just right to it ? As to being impractical because of the difficulties in a com- plex industrial system; to determine what an individual really does- produce, I cannot see any insurmountable difficulty In this. He asks,for example, what the respective reward of clerics and scavengers should be?' 1 answer : what the community which requires these services is propared,to give in exchange. The principle is the same if we go back • to the earliest conditions when the system of barter was in vogue. Suppose one man grows corn, another tends sheep. The first will :soon find it to his advantage to exchange his surplus corn, which the shepherd requires for for some of the, wool which the agriculturalist requires for. clothing. But the two may not always walat to exchange that which represents equal labour value. The shepherd may have more surplus wool than is represented by the surplus in the hands of the corn- grower. Hence the convenience of coinage which is current value between all-who want to exchange. As indus- trial operations became more complex the practice of barter prove i inconvenient and money became the sole medium and measure of exchange. The fruit or value of a man's labour is therefore measured by what it will exchange for, what it is worth of the labour of the other party to- the exchange, measured always by

the Common medium money. The equity of this will be acknowledged provided there is equality of opportunity, for everyone to exercise his power to produce. Lithe above case we presume that each party has free access to nature, the great storehouse of the materials in which alone we can store the results of labour. But suppose, an exclusive right is allowed to certain individuals to monopolize nature, its land, waters, minerals &e., then the workers of all classes and kinds are at a distinct disadvantage and have to make terms with those who have secured an individual right by which they can prevent access to a portion of the land, and only grant it on a payment in the form of a rent. It is clear that rent, of an individual character, conflicts with the ideal of a loan getting the whole fruit of his labour, because off the whole he has to pay away part in the form of rent for the right of access. In speaking of rent I mean true rent, access to nature apart from labour put upon land in buildings or improvements. Until the land monopoly can be ab dished and the State control the land of the country for the 14enefit of of all, and rent be paid to the State or municipality for access, there canwe be equality of opportunity. We stand for the justice of the individual having the sole right to the product of his labour, but to nothing else, asid inasmuch as man has not produced any natural element then our principle precludes the right of one individual to benefit over his fellow through exclusive possession of natural elements. The fact that the possession of natural things has been long recognised, been exchanged, been bought by the products of labour, has immensely complicated the question as far as putting the matter straight is con- cerned. But in equity no one has a right to be in a position to derive from Mother Nature anything but his individual share of her gifts. It is because capital has entrenched itself behind the land monopoly that it has become a menace. The problem which President Roosevelt is facing so bravely in his fight with Standard Oil and like Corporations arises out of the land monopoly. The Standard Oil has monopo- lized practically all known American oil bearing districts. The United States has allowed one of Nature's products to become private property and has now to face the result of this folly. Sweep away the land monopoly and you go far to reduce capital to the position of a servant of labour, instead of being what it now is to a great extent —its master. Only when this is done will a man be able to reap the full reward of his toil. "Perplexed" say. "surely Mr. Parker- does net believe that young children and industrial veterans have not a legitimate claim on Society ? " Certainly I admit the claim, but (at any rate in regerd to children) the State should stand second. The first deka is on the parents. Just as all young life depends for its support on that which brought it forth until it can fend for itself, so should the child depend neon the parents. This is nature's method and we deviate from it when the State assumes responsibilities which naturally belong to the parent. The feeding, nursing, and rearing of a child should be in the home where love glorifies and sanctifies a parent's service. But if the parents cannot, or will not, dis- charge their natural duties, obey their natural instincts, then for the sake of the children and the well-being of the State,- the nation must assume the position of foster parent. The present tendency and direction of legislation is inevitable because of the past follies and present conditions, but the municipal creche, with its official caretaker and its sterilized milk, is a poor and sad substitute for the mother's lullaby over the home cradle and the loving pressure of a baby face to the breast. Then in regard to industrial veterans, if a man get the full reward of his labour there would be ample margin to enable everyone to be independent in old age. But how can we exnect a man earning twenty shillings a week to feed, eloth,, brmke a family of five children in the standard of decency which our highly developed society demands, and at tl,e sitne time save for declining years. Let any who thinks it can be done try it. Ido not believe that the abolition of all monopoly would do away with all the ills that burden society. Sin in its various forms must, as long as it exists, produce sorrow and hardships. And it is well that it is so; the ways of transgressors a.ee made hard in order that the transgressors may be turned away from

===== 0002601_19071109_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
-

the ways-. But I fully recogni§e that much of the sorrow and, hardship is the result of conditions for which many wha have to bear very heavy burdens are not responsible. Our aim must be to give equality of oppertunity, and this being done to allow the individual to make the bast use of his opportunity. The mare a man can be left t) develop himself (pro- viding he has a fair field) the stronger will that individual become, and as a consequence the stronger will the State be:,-)lne of which he forms a unit. While on the other hand is the danger, to which it is folly to close our eyes, that State help carrie3 with it a tendency to weakea individual exertion and initiative by lessening the necessity for individual effort. Ido not know if " Perplexed " will cmsider that my letter bane his somewhat peremptory demand for a "prompt and satisfictory reply." I am not always satiaed with my own reasoning, and no doubt what I have said will raise many more questions. The subject is a large and difficult one and no one can adequately treat it in the brief compass of a letter. Besides this we are all mere seekers after 4ruth. The individual thinks he has found it, the Socialist feels sure he his. But these are different seekers and have only found a small broken arc of the ,great circle of truth. Perhaps by landiurr' these are and making our little curves true we may be surprised to find that they must meet ani form a larier arc. But we are still a long way off the time when the circle will be complete and trufh will be full orbe,d. Perhaps it is well that it is so !

CUARLES E. PARKER. Penketb, November sth,-1097.

A. CONUNDRUM.

•A friend of mine handed me a cutting from a local newspaper the other day, and J have come to the conclusion that it is about tim3 Sozialists stopped their propaganda ad turned their thoughts to Toryism. The article was a leader upon a specla which Mr. P. Snowden ha I made upon Old Age Pensions. Mr. Snowden tells how he would raise the money by an additional tax on all incomes over g,3,00) a year. This is simple enough on paper,says our brainy writer, but gr. Snowden has forgotten one thing. lbw is he I going to raise the money when the rich have ceased to I be rich?

There you are, answer that if you can. Under his system tha big incomes will clearease every year. II)w grald, how glorious, g nng to decrease their incomes by dropping rents and raising wages. Of course I take it to be so. Again he says : —Under the present system of over-taxing, the rich peaple have to sell or mortgage property or to reduce expenditure' discharge employees in order to meet the taxa- tion of the Death Ditties. When the rich cease to be rich, we shall have SJcia?isnt ; the people will have done allowing rich idle landlords and ! other idle drones to take two-thirds of the production without giving some equitable return. Under Mr. Snowden's system big incomes will decrease every year, says this scribe. That's what I want to see. His last paint is 3 tit-bit for sure. I have always understood people were taxed on their income but according to him they are taxed and then have to sell or take to Uncle's some of their ppssessions to raise the wind. Also I understand the Death Duties are put upon the value of the estate the deceased leaves behind. But I must be mistaken.

I will return to this subject agiin next week (with your permission, lfr. Editor) and prove that the aged poor ought to have a pension in order that they may be able to end their days in decency and comfort.

For instance why do no_t they hurl at the-head of the Socialist the following awful problem. W-no will clean the top store windows when it is ruining cats and dogs in a Socialist state? (I. hope this_ pr)blem has not flabergaved you gentle reader.) Surely these tub-thumping trilby hutted street cover barvers would pause and think for the first time in their lives when this gigantic problem would sound equally well when stated through the tin funnel of a gratnap'bone as through the funnel of a threohrindred a year orator. Wesleyan Circuit Leaders' Meeting.

According to the requirement of the Conference, says the "Warrington Circuit Wesleyan Methodist Church Record," a united meeting of the Class Leaders of the circuit has been held at Bold-street Church and proved very profitable. The consecration meeting in particular condUcted by the Rev. T. Miller,in the interval between the tea and the evening meeting, proved a memorable time, and it would be well if it were repeated in every class in the circuit. To give a detailed report of the address would be impossible. Several of our most successful leaders gave an account of their experiences and of the methods which have prospered in their hands. Rev. W. Mellor and Mr. Upson laid stress on the use of the Scripture in the class. They found it good to. give a short exposition to start the members on a train of thought and give variety to their utterances. Miss Dutton showed tha value of the class as an instrument of Christian servioe, and thought it should be much more uzed for that end. Mrs. Parkinson showed the beauty and [tower of entire surrender to the Master'st service.

THE SOCIAL QUESTION. I THE REV. SIDNEY PITT'S PLEA FOR

BETTER CONDITIONS. A Word About Socialist Proposals.

The. Rev. Sidney Pitt preached at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church on Sunday evening on" Religion and the Social question." Taking for his text the words " He that bath this hope in him purifieth himself even as God is pure" (John iii., 3) he said: One of the most striking signs of the times is the interest that seems to be universally taken in what is called the social question. There is no wonder that this is so. It ought to be so. The condition of the people of England question, as Carlyle foresaw, was the crying question of his time. It has been waiting quite long enough and it is likely to overshadow every other for some time to come. Unfortunately there are those in very opposite camps who are endeavouring to set Christianity against the legitimate claims and aspirations of the people. Extreme Tory and extreme Radical are both in opnosite directions falline into this mistake. The one is endeavouring to identify religion with the maintenance of class privilege and social injustice the other is attacking religion on account of its supposed indiffer- ence to the people's needs. Some of the manifestations of this tendency are very peculiar. When brewers for example and those who are closely associated with their business manifest a great ENTHUSIASM FOR RELIGIOUS EDUCATION the exhibition would be grotesque anfi ridiculous if it were not so utterly nauseous. But on the other hand the attack from the Radical side is a very bitter and envenomed one, and is doing a great deal at the present moment to alienate what are called the working CiaSSes from religion, and a greater disservice than that could not possibly he done them. We are told that religions people have been sitting in churches and chapels and singing "Glory be to God" and forgetting the con- dition of their brother and sister in the slum. We are told that parsons and others have been -prating of heaven and ignoring the bell that has been existing at their doors. Perhaps it is as well to admit to begin with that there is to) much truth in all this. A widespread statement of that sort would not have gained the currency it has gained unless there had boen some element of truth in it. It is not true of all. T dare venture to say it is not generally trap, hut there is or has been too much truth in it. In so far as there is any truth in it at all we as Christians should make haste to purge ourselves from any such necusition. We slsould spend and he spent to show that it is not true, for nothing assuredly could be more contrary to the spirit and teaching of our Lord and Master than that His servants should pass by on the other side while the aenostic or Socialist Samaritan, if such there he, attends to the man in the ditch. Tf there be professing Christians who are thus negligent then they are unfa,thful and inconsistent Christians. Christ and His apostles expressly disown them and unless they repent will utterly and ffaally disown them at the lest day. Further than this Christianity holds no brief for the evisting order of thins. There is a great deal existing in this professeily Christian country with which Christianity his nothing whatever to da except ts protest against it and alter it as son, as possible. To mention only one thing where it would he possible to mention very many; the way that children are tieing brought up—dragged up or rather dragged daam would he the titter wor.l—is a scandal and a shame to Christianity and a fresh wound to the Lord :Jesus Christ who died for them. Whoever is responsible for the dirt and crowding and

DRINK AND POVERTY fiq it exists in the lower ptrts of our large towns has a terrible ric!«ning to face and neither political economy nor anything else will excuse them when they come to stand as we all shall stand beforl the jndgment seat of Christ Himself. Hut now having admitted all this which I an sorrowfully compllled to admit, let me gl on to sag what needs to be said as to the position of 'Christianity in relation to the social problem of to-day. I venture to say this, that so fares the past. is concerned Christianity has done more for the social and temporal well-being of the people than anything and everything -else that has ever appeared upon the earth. What may happen in the future we none of us very .clearly know. Perhaps those who know the most are not the readiest to prophesy but to indge from the past at any rate the best friend the workingman has ever had 'has been the \fan of Nazareth and the best hope for the race as a whole, especially for the poor and degraded of it, is in the revival and spread of His teaching and His spirit. Therl are many proposals of social ameliora- tion in the air. I era not discussing them now. Fully and freely T admit the necessity for

===== 0002601_19071109_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
DEEP AND FAR REACHING CII ANGE.

of some sort in the condition of the body politic. But this Ido say : that Christianity is the ladder that we have risen by hitherto and we shall be fools to kick it sway at any rate until we have some certainty as to where we shall put our foot. The movement for the abolition of slavery came out of the very heart of -evangelical Christianity—Wilberforce was one of- the . most thorough-going Christians that ever.....lived—the father too o a geneVation of eminent Christians which lasts among us still. Where did the factory acts come cram ?—those beneficent provisions which have cast the aegis of their protection over the weakest members of our industrial system and which require in my judg- ment to he greatly extended. Lord Shaftesbury, the "bead and leader of that movement, was a man of the most intense and decided Christian character. And in both these instances the movement was precisely along -the line of Christian principles. It was their C'nristianity that impelled these men to do what they did. Similarly as to the position of women and chil- dren in the c wamunity in general. To-day wo have Itfuller's orphanage, Dr. Stephenson's children's homes, Dr. Barnardo's llomes,with their ever-open floor so that mo littie helpless child is ever turned away. Christian -men every one of the rt,not nominally merely but intensely 'Christians over all, impelled by their Christianity -to do what they do. And where do they go for their support? • Where- 6et. to Os despised church and. chapel people whose religion is supposed to be nothing else 'but the tolling Imof our eyes and the singing of psalms. For net only the orphanages but the hospitals ton -depend mainly upon Christian contributions for their -support. I know that other or4inisationa have come into the matter. in recent years but I suppose that still -these

-would have to close their doors if the collections of 'Christian congregations were withdrawn, that is unless -some other people gave in a way we are not do;ng now. It would be a very great pleasure to me to follow out this argument in detail throughout the centuries of our -era. I should have to make some large omissions and -some humiliating Confessions but T should not fear to undertake to prove that throughout the ages wherever ,a genuine and living Christianity has been, there has been a social uplifting such as at any rate has been nowhere else: If. anyone cares to pursue the matter In detail, I could put them in the way of the books where the facts are to be found. In the meantime let me give an illustration, before I pass on, of the way in which the religion of Jesus actually works .in helping the helpless and diAtressed. The • preacher then referred to the condact of Dr: Pusey, Regius professor of Hehrew at ()vier], who at the • time of the cholera offered his service as assistant curate to the incumbent of an East End parish and actually worked as -such, making use of his knowledge of Hehrew for the instruction in sanitary matters of the Jews among whom the epidemic prevailed. And now so much for the past. Such have been the , 'tendencies, snch have been the fruits of Christianity, .such fruits its it seems to me it is calculated to produce and must produce wherever it is fairly 'lived and gets its chance. But what about the present ? Here we are confronted with a condition of things around us which -as I should suppose nobody can defend. If anybody can defend the social condition as at present in some of these neighbouring str eta be is altogether outside the -scope of any argument of mine. 1,,f you think you can defend it, it is because yon haven't gone to look at it. and what can lest be done. There are certain proposals . made in the name of u'bat is cal!ed Socialism. I am not dkcussing them at the present moment. Some ig them I would hold-up both hands for at once. Some things that have been proposed in the name of Socialism should consider it, my duty to resist at the stake if it were necessary. There is this, however, to le said of the most of them. Whether they be good, bad or indifferent they are not wrhin arm's length of ns.There is the House or Lot ds to reckon with, and the House Of 'Commons too and the swing of the pendulum and the nest election and the nest election after that. be low before we see any great alteration. But here in the New Testament is a method of alleviation and reform which can be brought into play at once. "He that bath this hope in him let him purify himself evon as God is pure." We touch everyone of us this social environment at some point or other. Wherever we do touch it if we claim to have any share in the Christian hope at all let us bring our Christian principles to bear upon it. Lot every employer pLrify himself. Let him take care that he does not make his money out of the health and life and bodies and souls of those who work for him. Let him not say and do by his underlings what he would be ashamed to say and do by himself. Let every workman act on the same principle. In the use of time and plant in interest in his work, and in his relations with his fellow workmen, let him do the fair thing by those captains and leaders of industry with !whose brains and leadership industry is not likely to be able to dispense with. Let every capitalist purify himself and every investor. As he is or ought to be unwilling to eat the wages of unrighteousness himself he should be equally unwilling to take those wages- when they are earned for him by the hand of others. Let every property owner purify himself. Let him not live in ease and laxitry on the revenues of slums where health is impossible and decency almost ecoally se. Let every tenant purify himself. Let him use all the fresh air that he can get and all the fresh water that is necessary so as not to aggr tvate either for himself or for others the conditions in which he finds himself,how- ever unfavourable they may be. Let every voter purify himself and when he does there will be

Let every minister purify himself and it will take us all our time for, in the judgment seat where we must all appear no-one will have to face a stricter inquisition than we.

CHRISTIANITY AND SOCIALISM.

Rev. H. Dunnico's ViOWS

The inaugural gathering of Kensington Men's Confer- ence, Liverpool, took place on Monday evening in Jubilee-drive Baptist Church, there being a crowded gathering. _ _ _ _

; The law. Herbert Dunnico presided, and gave an ; address on "Christianity and Socialism: Are they Antagonistic ?" In the course of his remarks- the speaker said that the capitalistic Prns was waging a great warfare against Socialbon with the desperation of object fear. For his part he was not surprised, and did Mot complain so long as it, fought with clean and legitimate weapons. He did not hesitate to arfirm that some of the weapons now being used against Socialism were as discreditable as they were idiotic, ad as dirty as in the long run they would prove to bo useless. For good or for evil Socialism was growing in strength not only in this country, but France, Germany, Russia, America, and every country in the civilised world. If it was an immoral system, if it did strike at the sanctity of the marriage tie, the sacredness of the, home, then the so mer it was spurned by every lover of humanity the better. 13 at he asserted unhesitatingly that such charges were absolutely void of sense, reason, and truth. The splaker then gave a detailed account of the moral and economic aspects of Socialism, and endeavoured to demonstrate that they were inseparably bound up with the spirit of Christ. Socialism might not materialise exactly on the lines laid down; it might not accomplish all that they hoped it would ; but that ought not to prevent them joining hands with men who were seeking' to make the city a garden, the country a paradise, and the whole world a fitting abode for God. The c'aurches might go, the political parties might go, the very nations of the world might go. but there was one-thing greater, deeper, and broader than them all —namely, the spirit of Christ, which was the spirit of brotherhood, and against that the gttes of hell would not prbvail.

THE MUMMA', SOCIETY'S NEXT CONCERT.

SOME NOTES GN ELGAR'S "DAM OF GERNITIUS."

The Words-3y Canon Stzvenson.

The Dream of Gerontins is a lyrical-dramatic poem, describing death and the after-life ,as Gerontius dreamed he experienced them. fie dreams that he is dying, experiences " this new feeling, never felt Lefore,' ejaculates short prayers and Calls upon those beside him to pray for him. Life ebbs and flows and with each returning rally of strength he prays and as his strength wanes, his friends pray for him. The agony of death comes ore' him, 'he gasps forth his last prayers and the chorus of friends rises on his behalf :

• "So to show Thy per, - Rescue this Thy servant In his evil hour." Death -comes, and the commendatory prayer is said

Co forth 0 Christian Soul from this World

Go on thy course And may thy place to day be found in peace." -

So ends Part I

In the Second Part the Soul of Geroutius has passed into the place of the departed. There is a sensation of freedom, of silence, of rest, and he recognizes that he is being borne onward, sfayed up by some invisible power. It is his Guardian Angel and the song of the Angel tells that his work is done and the child of earth committed to his care has been brought in safety to the God Who gave him.- There ensues a dialogue between the Angel and the Soul in which the Soul seeks to realize how it is that all fear has passed from him, and the Angel explains that t!:e process • of judg- ment has begun and that a presage of acquittal has already passed into the Soul and given it peace. As they arrive close on the judgment court they hear the howl of tho demons assembled there: "Hungry and wild, to claim their property , And gather souls for hell." In an expressive chorus they pour forth their hate and scorn.

The next question of the Soul is whether he shall be permitted to see the Lord. The Angel tells him that he shall see Him for one moment, and explains that the sight will gladden but will also pierce. Purifica- tion must precede the Beatific Vision. As they approach the House of Judgment they hear the Choir of Angelicals singing their hymn :

" Praise to the Holiest in the height, And in the depth be praise: In all His words most wonderful: Most sure in all Ms ways I" &e.

The Ancel warns the Soul that his judgment now is near,and that he is drawing nigh to the veiled presence of God.

At this tt wful moment he hears the sound of the prayers of his friends on earth and as he passes into the presence of the Judge, the voices are heard in supplication:

" Be merciful, be gracious ; spare him, Lord. Be merciful, be gracious; Lord, deliver him."

The awful moment comes and the Soul realizes that there is much purification needed before he can live and abide in that presence.

"Take me away and in the lowest deep There let me be, And therein hope the lone night watche, keep, Told out for ms." And the chant of other souls arises: " Lewd, Thou bast been our refuge in every generation: Before tbs.hllis were born, and the.world was ; From age to age, Thou art Cod." The Angel comm;ts the Soul to his perild of purifi- cation, with words of loving comfort:

" Farewell, buena for ever ! brother dear, Re brave and patient on thy bed of sorrow; Swiftly shall pass thy ni.rht of trial here, And I will cn:ne and wake thee on the morrow."

And with tlr.) iepetition of the Angelical hymn the poem closes.

Cardinal Newman was a greater writer of prose than of poetry,but that he had some gift of poetry this poem proves, and two of his hymns certainly will live and be sung for generations to come, the one that we have already quoted and Ltd. kindly light." The solemn and mysterica“ subjec!, of the poem makes us feel that we are treading on holy ground. It is npt my province to speak of !he music which Sir Edward Elgar has wedded to the wcrds. That is in more competent hands ; • but one cannot but feel that it must have needed .some courage to have made the attempt. Thnse who have the pleasure of hearing this work at the forthcatnirw concert of our Musical Society will be aware that much time and pains hive been, expended upon a worthy production of it. No doubt the performance will sustain, if it does nt t enhance, the re:utatioa of the Society, and the audience will have an excellent opportunity of judging how far the composer has succeeded in a very dilicult task.

The Music—by Frank H. Crossley.

Sir Edward Elgar (horn 1857) first entered the field of musical composition as a ‘vriter of part songs and pieces for violin and pianoforte. His first important choral works were a short oratario " The Light of Life," " King Olaf" (twice performed by the Warringt in Musical Society), aid " Caractacus.' The ,litelity and success of these works was -so pronounced that great interest and expectatiens were aroused in the musical world when it became known that Elgae had undertaken the musical setting of Car linal Newmmarvellous poem "The Dream Of Ilerontins." The work was finished in the summer of 1000, and was first produced at the Birmingham Festival in October of that year. Unfortunately the performance on that occasion was so unsatisfactory owing to the shortcomitige of the chorus, that the productien quite failed to give an adequate idea of the beauties of the work. But, fortunately, while a certain amount of indifference and hesitation existed in this country, in Germany the work was hailed as a master- piece. The words sere translated into German and a splendid performance was given at the Lower Rhine Festival in 1911. In the f Ilowing year the Sheffield Festival followed with a fele performance, and eince then " Gerontius " has been givrn be nearly all the great choral societies of the Kingdom. The work, however, is one to be by no means. lightly undertaken owing to the extraordinary difficul- ties and complexities of the score. indeed it is well-known that More than one prominent society has almost given up the work in despair! These intricacies exist chiefly in the extremely elaborate writing for both the chorus and orchestra. In most ed the standard oratorios the chorus is seldom divided into more than four parts. In " Ger ,ntius "the voices are divided into eight, twelve and occasionally thirteen distinct parts ! In ad litioa tithe usual fall chorus, a . semi-chorus of sixteen or eighteen voice is employed. , This semi-chorus is used with most be lutVul effect, sometimes alone, and sometimes in conjunction with the general body ef voices, in in tny portions of the work. Then main, the orchestral wriiing is planned on a most extensive scale. A larger number than usual of wind instruments is employed in addition to which the strings must be very numerous, as in some places they ere sul; divided into as many as eighteen parts! The question may be asked with regard to all these difficult:es and complexities, whether the end is worth the means? The ansis.er should be emphatic- ally "Yes ;" for notwitlistarding the enormous amount of intricate detail, the effect - though freeinently quite different from .anything one .has ever, heard before—is always perfectly .natural and a-entrained. .The listeeer who hears the work for the first time. must be prepared for methods quite different from those usually adopted in the conventional form of oratorio writing. There are no set solos with full closes allowing the singers time to bow their acknowledgements during the applause which is frequently such a disturbing element ie an oratorio performance. The work is divided into two la:Ws and the music of each of these , parts flows on without a break. The melodies many of them of rare beauty, are never spun out, but are each one of them used to depict some particular emotion. Most of these melodies or themes recur again and again in different forms whenever the same emntion • or thought is suggested by the poem. But perhaps the moss notable point of all is the absolute sincerity of the music. No one can pos6bly doubt the deeply reverent manner in which the composer has undertaken his gigantic tesk. The Prelude at once sugg sts a mystical atmospheie which is a I strong characteristic ef the entire ivork. It is of con- Isiderable length and importance and contains most of the principal themes which are 'afterwards heard in the bedy, of the work. The Prelude leads without break into the first tenor solo (Gerontius). Realising that his end is at hand Gerontius appeals to his friends (Assistants) - "Pray for me ray friends who have not strength to pra,y." • The semi-chorus is introduced at this point with most ethereal effect as they whisper the " Kyrie." Gerontius then has a short solo "-Rouse thee my fainting soul and play the

man," which is followed by a most beautifully tender and supplicating chorus : " Be, merciful, be gracious, spare him, Lord" The lollies( solo in the work then follows in which Gerontius gives expression to his last confession of faith. This is broken by a most wcnderful orchestral inte:lnde descriptive of the mental and spiritual feelings of the sufferer. Towards the close of this solo at the words "And tferse, and worse, some bodily form of ill floats on the wind with many a loithsome curse," a refer- ence is made to the "demon" mu,ic which figures prominently in Part 11. After Gerontius' last despair- in,' appeal " 0 Jesu help," the full chorus follows with "Rescue him, 0 Lord." This chorus wh.ch com- mences with a species of religious frenzy, soon sub- sides into a more supplicating tone and leads into a Litany of extraordinary beauty and impressiveness. rile semi-chorus chant the verse in unison accompanied by the organ on an anc'ent " tone," and the full chorus comes in at intervals with the "Amens" -an instance of a great effect obtained by a simple means: The bushed chorus then pray "Rescue this Thy servant in i his evil hour," after which the soul of Gerontius takes 1 its flight as he sings the words "Into Thy hands " ! 1 the last chord of the music and the prayer itself being unfinished. After the extreme solemnity of the fore- going passages it is something of a relief to hear the fine sonorous chords which announce the • Priest's charge "Go forth upon thy journey, Christian soul!" This solo (bass) is afterwards amplified by the chorus with great breadth and .dignity. T-he voices then divide into several parts and a passage of remarkable and ethereal beauty bring S the first secti .n to an end. It has been suggested that the crossing of the vocal parts, while a low D is sustained on the irgan in this passage, suggests the waving of golden censers. Part IL opens with a beautiful introduction for muted strings suggestive of the soul's passage thrnugh space led by the Angel's guiding hand. Some lovely music here accompanies the dialogue between the Soul (tenor), and the Angel (mezzo-soprano) 'until at the words (Soul) "But hark! upon my sense comes a fierce hubub " the peaceful strains are interrupted by strangely harsh and weird sounds. It is the demons and their distant howls. " Low born clods of brute earth, they aspire to become gods " : - and later " What's a saint? One whose breath doth the air taint before his death: A. bundle of bones which fools adore, Ha ! ha ! when life is o'er." The musical setting of these words is probably tbe most dar;ng effort in choral writing ever achieved or attempted, and the composer's grip of the subject here is truly remarkable. The chorus and orchestra vie with each other in hurling out the most vehement and discordant phrases, and the scene is awe-inspiring in the extreme, and full of bold grandeur. At length the Soul and the Angel continue their journey, and the mocking jeering voices of these terrible demons are left further and further in the distance until they finally die away. The dialOgue is then resumed for a brief period until the Soul and the Angel arrive at the abode of the ..A.ngelicals, and here the music undergoes a remarkable change, and as the first words of " Praise to the Holiest in the height" are sung, strains of serene and celestial beauty are heard. , This number is developed at considerable length, the voices are divided into several parts, and are gradually worked up until the whole mass of voices and orchestra unite in producing a magnificently grand and triumphant climax. Again the Angel and the Soul resume their dialogue (Angel) "Thy judgment, now is near ;" (Soul) " I hear the voices that I left on earth." But this is interrupted by a very striking and impressive solo for the "Angel of the Agony "(bass) -" Jean, by that shuddiring dreld which fell on Thee." In this solo the comp)ser shows a supreme disregard of one of the elementary rules of musical grammar-" con- secutive fifths abound, -but the result is always perfectly satisfactory and indeed very beautiful: thus once again proving that genius rises superior tl mere pedagogic rule! We then hear the voices on earth singing as in the first part at the bed-side of Gerontius "Be merciful, be gracious : spare him Lord." A marvellous orchestral passage then indicates that the Angel's promise is fulfilled. "Yes, for one moment th Ar shalt see thy Lord." There is a note in the composer's score at this point, "for one moment must every instrument here exert its fullest force." A short chorus of souls in Purgatory leads to the wonderful Finale, which, as the original analyst suggests "puts into the shade ,verything that has gone before." The Angel here bids farewell to the Soul, and the Music contains several allusions to what we have previously beard, but- everything seems idealised. More tender and touching music has seldom been written. It is music which comes straight from the heart, and must surely reach the heart of every sympathetic listener. And so the work ends-a work full of noble dignity, full of emotional expression, arid full of deep sincerity. That the composer has put forth his most earnest endeav mrs is amply proved by the fact that at the end of his M.S. score he has written the words : "This is the best of me."

THE TRANSIT OF MERCURY.

THE APPROACHING TRANSIT OF THE PLANET HER:alr ACROSS THE DISC OF THE SUN. [By a Fellow of the Royal Astronomical Society.] Of the eight principal 'planets that are known to revolve round the centre of our system, the Birth, which is the dwelling place of man is the third in order counting outwards from the sun. There are therefore two planets which revolve within the ellipse in which our earth moves and it sometimes happens that these bodies get in a direct line between us and the sun, and appear to us as round black spots moving slowly across the disc. Mercury, the planet nearest the sun makes 191 revolutions in 40 years, and Venus the next planet, 13 revolutions in eight years very nearly. Both orbits are inclined at a considerable angle to the orbit of the earth, and it is only when one of the planets and the earth are together in I particular place near what is called the node, or the point where the plane of one orbit intersects that of the other, that a transit across the sun's disc becomes possible. Transits of Venus are rare and occur •in pairs after long intervals of time, the sequence of their occurrence being 8,120, 8,105 &c. years the firt pair of transits were observed in the years 1874 and 1882 and the next pair will take place, in 2001 and 2012. . Transits of Mercury are more frequent, twelve., to fourteen occurring in a century, the last one was observed in the year 1894, and there will be another cn Thursday next the 14th of the present month. They are not always visible in this country as they some- times take place when the sun iS bdow our horizon, but in the present case the phenomenon is clue at noon and Will be well seen here providel the sua is n-,t obscured by clouds at he time. The phenomenon will not be visible to the .naked eye,,but a small telescope or a good opera glass should be sufficient to show it ; 'care being taken to protect the eye from the effect of the sun's glare by placing a shade of coloured glass over the eyelens. The calculated time when the trnnsit begins is, for aby Part of Lancashire 23 minutes and 25 seconds ' past ten o'clock in the morning of the 14th, the neorest approach of the planet to the sun's centre will occur at seven minutes past twelve o'clock, and the planet will pass off the disc at 50 minutes and 51 secands past one o'clock. Greenwich time. The time occupied in crossing the disc being nearly tine° hours and a half.

There are certain Changes known to be taking pl in the orbit of the planet, the physieal cause of which l has not yet been ascertained, and. astronomers will observeithe transit and note any discrepancy tint rniy be found between the actual and the computed times of contact, and may do something towards solvin,_ some-of the outstanding problems connected with the planets movements. • The appearance presented by the __planet whilst crossing the sun's disc will also be carefully watched by those in possession of adequate instrumental means, .and anything that may lead to an indication of the planets physical nature carefully noted. Formerly transits of the inferior 'planet especially those of .Venus were considered the best means of finding the distance between the earth and the sun, but owing to the great difficulty experienced in obtain- ing exact observations of this class of phenomena r these have now given. place .to methods susceptible of much greater accuracy.

THE NEW SILCOATES SCHOOL.

Foundation S ones Laid.

At Silcoates, near Wakefield, on Saturday. the foundation stones were laid of the new Northern Congregational School, which is to take the place of the premises burned down during the Easter recess of 1904. Since the disastrous fire—reminders of which were still to be seen on Saturday in gaunt, ruined wens —Silcoates School has been carried on at Saltburn, where a vacant convalescent home was secured. The designs selected for the new school were those of Messrs. Oliver and Dodgshnn, of Leeds. The stone-laying ceremony was performed under a marquee, in which the visitors were accommodated on a platform. Mr. J. Compton Rickett, M.P., chairman of the Congregational Union, presided, and among others present were Mr. Mark Oldrosd, Alder.nan G. H. Baines (West Hartlepool), Mr. J. G. H. Howorth (Nlan- chester), Mr. Basil 5, Briggs (secretary), Mr. J. A. Yonge (head master), am. the Rev. Gritfith-Jones (Principal of the Yorkshire United College, Bradford). Mr. Briggs also announced that the total subscrip- tions amounted to £4,472. Alderman Baines, chairman of the Governors, in further explaining the financial position. said that the decision to come back to the old site had been welcomed by the whole denomination. (Applause.) Had a site been selected elsewhere, as at first proposed, a capital of £25,000 or £30,000 would have been needed, whereas at Silcoates the shell of the new school would cost only .ClO,OOO. Furniture and incidentals woull probably bring the cost up to .C 12,000 or £13,000. It was hoped that by the end of next year the subscription list would reach £7,000, and it would be an easy matter to raise the rest by mortgage for a brief period. The school estate bad recently been valued at £10,500. (Applause.) The Chairman said that it might be said by some that in the atmosphere of to-day a large schoal such as that should be in one of the " 'Varsity" towns, brit be was persuaded that in the development of education there was rnom for a school of the Silcoates type. Proceed- ing, he remarked that the religious instruction of the school was in no way antipathetic to that of other denominations.

The foundation stones were then laid—one on behalf of Yorkshire, another for Lancashire, and the third for the governing body. Mr. T. C. Howarth, in laying the stone for Lanca- shire, said it was hoped that the school would be opened a year hence free from mortgage, so that any income from the estate might go to widenieg the opportunities of the school. (Applause.) At least £lO,OOO more was wanted, so that there was plenty of scope for "pious founders." (Laughter.) When the school was opened an extension of the scholarship scheme would be desir- able. (Applause.) A vote of thanks to the Chairman and to the gentle- men who had laid stones was moved by Mr. H. Higson (Blackburn), who said that in Lancashire and Cheshire there was a goodly number of old Silcotians. Principal Griffith-Jones seconded the resolution, and said he earnestly hoped that the necessity for raising money by mortgage would not arise. (Hear, hear.) The resolution having been supported by Mr Yonge, the headmaster Of the school, was adopted. The Chairman, in responding, drew attention to the fact that no jars, containing coins and newspapers, had been placed beneath the stones. The building of a church was often made the opportunity of burying church jars, but the Governors of Silcoates School had no jars to bitty. (Laughter.) Mr. Mark Oldroyd also responded, and this ended the proceedings.

ILY DEAR CIIILDREN,

You will all be sorry to hear that I am laid up with influenza, and really feel too ill to write you a letter. Next week you must look out for the names of prize-winners, as I hope to be well by then.

Your affectionate • UNCLE NED.

With love from, Yours affectionately, HAROLD HALL.,

My Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending you a piece of poetry, which I hope to see in print:—

MY RAG DOLL.

I have a lot of dollies, Old and new, and large and small; Some are dark and some are fair, One has lovely golden hair; All the rest of them are finer, But I love my black doll Dinah, My rag dolly, best of all. She isn't very pretty, -- Nor beautifully dressed, But I love her more than money, Papa though* it very funny, Laughed, and called it quite a joke, When I said, "She can't be broke— That is why I love her best." But my darling mamma Smiled, and said, "The child is right, We should keep our thoughts and love Fixed on things that are above, Things that cannot be destroyed, Then all sorrow we'd avoid, And dwell for ever in the light." IDA. Bow,

Looking wistfully at the dish for a moment, she thought she saw her way cut of the difficulty, and exclaimed, "Well, give me anozzer piece, and send for ze doctor."

_ Dear Uncle Ned,—lt is a long time since I wrote before, and I am now sending you a short story, entitled, "The Three Friends." I enjoy reading the "Children's Corner" very much, and - especially the poetry. I am learnini two more pieces of poetry from the " Comer' to recite at our "Band of Hope" entertainment, which takes place in November. I should like to see my story inserted in the " Corner " soon. From your loving niece, CLARRIE HEAP. - THE THREE FRIENDS. They all lived in a little country village. The old man's name was Dave Bedingfield; he h.ad once been a sailor, but had lost one of his legs through an accident, and when I first got to know him he had been living in the little. rose-covered 'cottage quite a long time, earning his living by selling cabbages which he grew in his garden.

Every morning he filled up his little cart, and Mary, the dpnkey, trotted gaily down the road to the village, Nelson, the dog, running along beside her, Dave sitting on the shaft, his wooden leg stuck out before him, his jolly red face beaming out on the world, for he was a merry old soul if ever there was one. So here we have the three friends—Dave, Mary the donkey, and Nelson the dog. But, alas, one autumn morning Dave woke up with a terrible pain in his well leg. c Then he tried to dress himself, but it caused him such pain that his cheery face grew most woeful. Nelson watched him with a world of trouble in his soft brown eyes. At last _Dave- sat down with a groan. "No go, Nelson boy," he said; "I can't do it. One leg gone, and t'other got stuck. That's a pretty piece of business if ever there was. What we are to do I don't know—no cabbages sold, no money for us." Nelson stood looking up at him, as he sat on. the little bench by the door, then his tail began to wag, and he went to Mary, and said some- thing to her which Dave could not understand. Well, no sooner had Nelson spoken than he jumped into the cart and took the reins in his teeth. With a short bark he shook.them, and, to Dave's astonishment, Mary trotted off to- wards the village. "Well, if they ain't goin' to do the job for me!" he said.

Down the village came Mary and Nelson, stopping before each well-known door. It was not long before all the cabbages were sold, and the two set off homewards, the cart strewn with coppers. This happened every day till Dave was better, but during cne of their mornings alone in the village Nelson met with a nasty accident. He had left the cart to have a drink, and on the way back he stood on a piece of broken glass. "I can't jump into the cart," Ire said, in despair, standing on three legs. Well, what do you think Mary did. She just went down on her forelegs, and told him to get on her back, and that was how she took him home

After this Nelson had to stay at home for a day or two, Dave and Mary taking the cab- bages; but, at last his foot healed, and one sunny morning the three of them went their rounds together.

THE ANGELS' WHISPER. A baby was sleeping, it's mother was weeping, For her husband was far on the wild, raging sea; And the tempest was swelling round the fisher- man's dwelling, And she cried,_ "Dermot, darling, oh! come

back to raeo' ERT beads while she numbered, the baby still

slumbered, And smiled in her face as she bended the

knee. • "Oh I blessed be that warning, my ohild, thy

_ sleep adorning, _ For I know that the angels are whispering

with thee; And while they axe keeping bright watch o'er thy sleeping, Oh, pray to them softly, my baby, with me. And say thou would'st rather they'd watch o'er

thy father, For I know that the angels are whispering

with thee." The dawn of the morning saw Dermot return-

jug, And the wife wept with joy her babe's father

to see; And closely caressing her child with a blessing, Said, "I knew that the angels were whisper- ing with thee."

_ GIVING WEIGHT. One day a grocer, in weighing out a purchase to a customer, searched in vain about :the scales for something. "Patrick!" he called out to his assistant, "where is the pound weight?" "Sure, Misther Jones has it" "What do you mean?" "An', sure, didn't ye till me to be perlite to rigular customers?" "Of course." "Well, thin! Mister Jones comes in the day for a pound o' tay. An', says he, whin I axed him what quality o' tay he wud have. ' What- ever ye give me,' says he, give me the weight!' So I put in the pound weight in the package wid the tay, perlite-like, an' it's himself that's gone wid it!'

Dr.Williams' Pink Pills

you can defy and cure all November ailments. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People purify and enrich the blood and so fortify the system against winter ills. In Countless instances this has been proved.

'4" About 12 months ago," said Miss Fletcher, living at 4, Brewery Street, Birmingham, "owing to the damp and chili), weather, I caught a shocking cold and then contracted Influenza. I felt heavy and languid and my eyes were watery and painful ; then my throat becari4e inflamed, and I found it difficult to breathe. Chilly tremours ran down my spine and I shivered in spite of the warmest clothing. Influenza got such a strong hold upon me that I lost the use of my limbs and became bedridden. A troublesome cough attacked me and sharp pains pierced my lungs and heart. "The cold affected my kidneys and caused cruel pains across my loins. Food was out of the question, and whatever I swallowed induced vomiting. Doctors said I should not recover. My cold had resulted in anaemia and debility. "Mother then purchased some Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for me. ===== 0002601_19071109_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FREE CHURCHES.

THE CONGO crasls.

There is likely to be a big agitation during the coming months with regard to the posi- tion of affairs on the Congo. For years now the evidence of horrors and violence has been accumulating, until the whole British public has realised that something must be done. Sir Edward Grey has made representations, and agitation and diplomacy have done their best; the result is at length revealed. After twelve months' wait the Belgian Government has published its proposals. Their nature is such as to constitute an insult to the intelligence of the British people. Under the proposed Annexation Law, King Leopold retains in every essential particular the entire legislative, executive, and financial control of the Congo. He draws up the laws, appoints (and dismisses) the official charged with their application, controls the finances, selects (and dismisses) the magistrates, selects the members of the "Colonial Council," and is even empowered to retain the "Congo State" flag in a Belgian Colony. No pro- vision is made for the rights of the native population, which are wholly and absolutely at his mercy, and at the mercy of his financial bodyguard.

FREE CHURCH ACTION.

The National Free Church Council has at once taken action in the matter, .and is imuing a strongly-worded appeal to Free Churchmen to organise and agitate against the proposals. In all probability, meetings will be ntld throughout the country during the autumn, when citizens will insist on the Government taking the most decided action in the matter.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
MUNICIPIL ELECTIONS

During the past few weeks the country has been- in the thick of the Municipal Elections. Free Churchmen are taking more and more interest in civic affairs, as it is realised that it is not sufficient to watch the making of Laws 'at Westminster, but that their local administration should be closely scrutinised. This year, for the first time, women are eligible for membership of Municipal Councils, and no doubt the result of the elections will show that many Free Churchwomen have been appointed to exercise their influence on local politics.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
THE QUESTION OF LOCALISING.

A modern Church organisation is far more complex than our fathers could have imagined. Of late years the Church has realised more and more the value of the Press, and has sought its co-operation in many ways. One of the most successful auxiliaries of Church work at -the present time is the localised magazine. Local Churches and Free Church Councils issue a magazine, the body of which is printed in London and the cover and local news added locally. This is issued for house- to-house visitation and for circulation among members, and, besides keeping the Church in touch with its members and with outsiders, it is often a source of financial profit. Of course, all depends on the character of the " inset " which is localised. Where this is cheaply produced, with printing, illustrations, and literary matter of an inferior character, its value to the Church is-questionable. ,The National, Flee Church Council, recognising the importance of supplying an inset of the highest quality, publishes the Hee Churehnzan, a maga- zine especially for localising. The success of this magazine has been extraordinary during the last few years, and at the present time the sales are no less than 120,000 pea. issue greater %ban five years ago.

A CHARMING PRODUCTION.

An advance edition of the January number of HI _ Free Yliurrkaan has been issued, -together with the programme for 1908. This January number is certainly a most charm- ing production. "IV. Scott Kink" (Rev. W. Kinascote Greenland) is contributing the serial story for the year, and a most interest- ing instalment is given in the January issue. Dr. Horton contributes a "Talk to Parents," under the title "His Little One." Rev. F. B. Meyer gives the first of series of "Lessons on Life," dealing this time with "The Tragedy of Money." Articles by Rev. Charles Brown on "Twenty-five Years in the Ministry;" the late Rev. John Watson, "The Harbour of Sweet Content ;" and Dr. Campbell Morgan, "If any Man Thirst," will be read with great interest. The illustrations throughout the number are most excellent, and every page appeals to the eye. The National Free Church Council will gladly send full particu- lars of the Free Churchman, with terms for localising, on application to the Publication Department, Memorial Hall, London, E.C.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE. CHURCH PIONEERS.

In view of the revival of interest in the great founders of our modern Free Churches, the National Free Church Council has pro- duced in colours, by a new process, six splen- did portraits of Oliver Cromwell, John Bun- yan, John Milton, John Wesley, George Whitefield, and George Fox. These are being presented free with every copy of the January number of the Free Churchman. It is antici- pated that there will be a great demand for this handsome portfolio, as the portraits are well worth framing.

MSESTABLISH3IENT.

Perhaps the most remarkable feature of the Church Congres: held at Yarmouth was the deliverance by the President, the Bishop of Norwich, on. Disestablishmcnt. Rarely_ has the case for disestablishment been presented so ably and convincingly. True, the Bishop, at the end of his address, gave some reasons for resisting disestablishment, but even these, on careful examination, seem only to conceal further arguments for that Measure.- 't his in out h's Free 'Church, Chronicle reprints the Bishop's address in, ertcnso, and he article will doubtless be of considerable service to Free Churchmen.

SITUATION IN WALIN,

The'political situation in Wales is decidedly better now than it has been for tome 'months past. The great Nonconformist Convention on the lli:,;establishment of the Church in Wales did much to dear the air. Whilst un- doubtedly the Tory Prcs did much to exag- gerate the alleged revolt, it:eininot be denied that consideraelec uneasineti had been Nt among loyal supportera of the Government a 3 to the prospects of dise,tablislinient.. Fortu- nately, Mr. Lloyd George, M.P., was able to give an emphatm declaration that a Disestab- lishment Bill in Woks would be submitted to the fourth Session of this Parliament, and pushed through all the stages. necessary to pass it through the flowv of Commons. The effect of the Convention can only be fully realised when the branches of the league formed under one of the re6ahltions of the day come to be established in every Parlia- mentary constituency of Wales aml Mon- meuthshire, the purpose of this being to carry out. a disestablishment propaganda, and to see that no Parliamentary candidate is supported who is not pledged to demand the immediate settlement of the Welsh Usestabl!.;iiment que6Lin.

The first meeting between Warrington and Salford this season took place at. Weaste on Saturday before 5,000 people. A capital game was witnessed, in which Watrington-had the best of matters and ought to have won. Teams and officials: Salford—A. Mason, back; Cook, W. The opening stages of the game were greatly in favour of Wgrrington, And but for the good defence put up by the home side the Wirepullers would undoubtedly have scored early on, Mason pulling down Kenyon in fine style. However, the first score came to the visitors, G. Themes placing a penalty goal, whilst, ahortly afterwards Tilley gathered and steered the ball betneen the uprights with a fine drop. Continuing to have the best of the game the visitors further added to their score, G. Thomas kicking another penalty and Cartwright also dropping a goal. Close on the ieterval Warwick crossed for Salford, Mason failing. On resuming the home side at once began to attack. but Tilley repeatedly got his side out of difficulties. However, John. after beating the opposition with the exception of Tilley, made a dash for the line, Let the Warringten back effected a siand tackle. Heath had to leave the field with an injured knee. Towards the close Buckler scored near the post., for Mason to land a goal. Result :—Salford 1 goal 2 tries (8 points), War- rington 4 goals (8 points). WIDNES v. WIGAN: A DRAWN GAME.

Half-time—Wigan, 1 goal (2 points), Widnes' nil.— Immediately on commencing the second half, Widnes attacked, and Davies placed a goal hem a mark. Shortly afterwards a fine breakaway by Lambert ended in Noon scoring a gcod try. Davits failed to improve. After this Widnes were almost continuously on the Wigan line, but they failed to add to their score. On the other hand Wigan broke away in the last minute, and Barr got over after some scrambling play. Shprrock failed at anal, and the game resulted in a d:aw of 1 goal and 1 try (5 points) each.

Shop-Door Theft.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Margaret Lynch, Old-road, pleaded gulity to steeling a pair of boots, valued at 38. 111 d., from outside the shop of Messrs. Briggs and Co., Market-gate, about 6.3,5 on Saturday night. The Chief Constable said prisoner was seen outside the shop about 6.30 by an assistant named Mr. Ainley. lie saw her koking A t the boot., and he asked her if she wanted to make a :purchase. She then walked round to Hersemarket-street, but returned a little later and placed her shawl over some hoots and took a pair away. -Mr. Ainley followed her, brought her back to the shop, and informed the police. Prisoner, who had not been before the Court. previously, was fired 10s. and costs (£1 95.) or 14 days.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for pnblication must ia all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday.

Mr Grosfield and" Nulli Secundus." Sir.—l observe that in last Saturday's issue of your contemporary a correspondent who signs himself " Insight " charitably insinuates that a letter which appeared over the signature of " Nulli Secundus," and ray answer to it, which you were good enough to print in the "'Examiner "were, in plain English, a "put up job "for political purposes. He is mistaken. With all his perspicacity he has discovered only a mare's next, as he will realise when he hears that Ido not even know who " Nulli Secundus" is. There was therefore no collusion between us when Nulli Secundus "wrote and asked me a polite and a straight question which I thought then, and think now, deserved an equally direct answer. "Insight," whose nom de plume by the way scarcely seems to have been happily chosen, will perhaps excuse me if I don't pur- sue this little controversy with him any further. We can at least, I presume, shake hands over the admirable settlement that has been arrived at—with honour to all parties concerned—saving the country from what would have been nothing short of a National calamity.—Yours, etc., A. H. CROSFIELD.

Hoylake, November.7tb, 1907. _ -

Proposed Benefit to D. Isherwood. Sir,—Probably you are aware that a special general meeting .of members to beheld in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-Street, has been called for Friday November 15th, to consider the proposed benefit to Mr. D. Isherwood the popular centre of the Warrington Rugby Football Club. I hope all members of the club will make a special effort to attend the meeting, and show their appreciation of Mr. Isherwood's valuable services to the club by granting the benefit, which in my opinion has been well earned. Yort..s truly, HARRY ROGERS, Feather's Hotel, Bridge-street, Warrington. Bth November, 1907. The Elections. _ _

&Ir.—The Municipal contest is now over, the Drink Trade is victorious, and we have suffered defeat. In the conduct of the campaign we have fought honourably and above board, we have fought a good fight, and as Temperance Reformers we are not ashamed or dismayed thqugh somewhat disappointed our motto ctill is nil despe ra ?Limo . The Brewing interest is the party responsible for the late contest and have chosen their own gror.nd by putting forward in every case representatives of the Trade ; not content to rest the contest on that one question,they have dragged in the question of religious education and better housing. Who ever beard of the Tratle advancing education or is it possible for them to improve the homes of the working-class? We have henceforth dangled before us the spectacle of Boniface clothed with a cloud of etheral blue grasp- inf with one hand a little ragged urchin whom he is going to better house and by the other a school boy whom he is going to better educate and teach him the way he ought to go. How will he do either? On the Council we now have four Brewers representing four different brewing concerns besides several publicans. The Council will in the near future have to deal directly or indirectly with the buying-out of licence-holders. How will they handle the question? Bear in mind we have some fine palaces to get drunk In and you have a fine palace in Arpley-street to get sober in, but I am afraid your insanitary dwellings will remain. I shall watch the process in the future and shall be very pleased if I find myself to he a false prophet.—l am, &c,

PHIL SQUOD.

Sir,—The heat of last Friday's contest has died down and I am in no way surprised to find that the electors are far from satisfied with their "bargain " and many who voted for "beer" now see only too plainly how their action has aroused the indignation of a large pro- portion of their fellow townspeople. It is somewhat remarkable what absurdly ridiculous bluff is put forward in order to successfully claim the vote of the working class. What splendid service was done by the three R's ' (Rogers, Roberts and Religious liberty). Do the electors suppose that a branch of the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel will be opened at Wilders- pool Brewery or the Feathers Hotel? The only manner we can expect them to "let their light shine" will be the gas lamps of their "glittering Gin Palaces." If these gentlemen have suddenly become religious, why could not Councillor Roberts show it by thanking from the Town Hall steps those who had been so generous as to place him in a position to pratect the interests of the firm he represents? The working man in Howley Ward had an abundance of friends up till 8 p.m. on Friday. But after going to the Park to hear the opinion of the" favoured few" be finds he has been "sold." We aro told it was a Conservative victory. But will any of the Conservative party be good enough to tell us why it is that their particular party only nominates crndidates connected with the drink trade, which is an insult to the teetotal Conservative voters? Rather is it not a party working for the propagation of this abhorred trade under the guise of so-called religion, flying a false flag in order to defeat the ends of those who are working for a sober and a righteous people? I am astonished that a section of the Christian Church can make such an unholy alliance,and utterly fail to see how their attitude is compatible with their creed. Do they ever ask themselves" What would Jesus do ?". I hope to write again at a later date. I am, yours faithfully, 7th November, 1007.

J. IL GRIFFITHS

JUNIOR LIBERALS "The Value and Significanca of the Hague Conference."

At a meeting of the Junior Liberal Association on Monday evening Mr. Harold Gray gave an address on the above subject. Mr. R. E. Burtt presided. Mr. GRAY contended that some wars had unquestion- ably done a certain amount of good in the past, but that civilisation, especially Enropean civilisation, had reached such a point in its development that war among European nations could be nothing less than retrogressive and disastrous. War enthroned force and could very rarely be said to equitably settle a quarrel. The Hague Conference showed a decided advance towards a better and saner method. With the help of education and civilisation it would be more difficult to obtain the consent of people to a war. Although the Conference did not make any great advance on any great single principle, notable advances were made in many directions. The reduction or check of armaments was not discussed, but he was , informed by_one_who was at the Hague that there was '

a widespread feeling among the dele-gates that this sub- ject should not be relegated to the background, but that efforts should be made in all possible directions to bring about the very desirable end. Even the principle of obligatory arbitration was notprominently discussed. The U.S.A. and our delegates tried to induce the Con- ference to accept the principle in certain secondary matters, but there were a few dissensions. The main lines of advance were in the systematizing. and hence the strengthening of international law. Hitherto chaos and confusion bad reigned, but out of chaos evolved n cortain amount of order, _

and this movement in itself would do much good. The position of neutrals during war was prominently discussed and this kind of work would facilitate progress, because it would be more difficult for a neutral (so-called) to lend aid to any of the bellicose parties. Food supply was considered and a notable move was made to the effect that an exchange of memoranda should take place between the various Governments respecting future naval construction. Hitherto, the war-like section of the Press bad most ingeniously worked up scares, but in future it would have less to feed upon. This ventilation of a painful subject must tend towards a better understanding. Forces growing in majesty, continued Mr. Gray, were aiding the struggle against war and militarism— humanithutility and Christianity. International arbitra- tion was already in the sphere of practical politics. Our vision of peace should be world-wide, embracing. all men. The proved practicability of this principle was amply shown in the moral progress of the last century. Those who defended war did it ln an apologetic tone. Let us do whatever lay in our power to further this great idea. War was inconsistent with the dictates of humanity and alien to the spirit of common fellowship

among men. A long and interesting discussion followed, Mr. A. TILLING, Mr. R. LLOYD-JONES, Mr. NlorrroN,Councillor HAWTHORN and others, including the CITAIRNIAN,taking part. General regret was expressed that a greater advance had not been made at the last Conference, but t was considered that the time was opportune for further discussion and a spreading of those great prin- ciples which still awaited definite crystallisation in an institution such as a permanent Court of International Arbitration, where all matters of primary and secondary interest should be judged by an impartial tribunal. Next Monday evening Councillor Hawthorn will speak on "Local Municipal Questions."

WARRINGTON CYCLING CLUB.

Dinner to Celebrate Twenty-First Anniversary.

Speech by Mr. A. H. Crosfield M.P.

The members of the Warrington Cycling Club are naturally proud of the fact that this year the organiza- tion has attained its twenty-first anniversary, and the event was celebrated at a dinner held at the Patten Arms Hotel on Thursday evening. The function was a com- plete success, the wheelers mustering in strong force, and there was ample indication that the club was never more prosperous and active than to-day. The esteemed president ( Mr. John Naylor) presided and was supported by the Mayca (Alderman Smethurst),Mr. A. H. Crosfteld, M.P., Mr. Robert Pierpoint, Councillor W. B. Roberts, Mr.F.Fieldhouse (captain),Sergeant Major Delmage(sub- captain), Mr. G. H. Smith (hon. secretary) and others. An excellent repast was served and afterwar3s the usual toast list was gone through. The loyal toasts were enthusiastically honoured on .the proposition of the President.

THE CLUB'S FIRST RUN. The HON. SECRETARY announced that he had received letters of apology for non-attendance from Mr. J. Charlton Parr, M. Roger C. Parr, Mr. T. Andley (chair- man of the Liverpool centre of the N.C.U.), Mr. Alfred Perris,Mr. Qharles Pugh (Liverpool) and the Manchester Wheelers C.C. Mr. Alfred Perris, who was the first captain of the club, wrote that he remembered leading the first club run out from the George Hotel yard and the days of his captaincy were still freskin his memory --NAVY, ARMY AND RESERVE FORCES. Mr. ROBERT PiEtRPOINT proposed this toast. He sometimes thought that people, especially those who were the non-fighting part of the community, were a little too apt to forget the services of those who were of the fighting community and who wore that uniform to which they ought to attribute every_honour. They were also a little !too apt to forget the great anniversaries in the nation's history. They should, even more than they did now, cherish in their minds the memories of what our fighting men had done in times gone by and they could not do that better than by remembering the days as they went by and what they celebrated.. Wednesday, for. instance, was the anniver- sary of one of the toughest battles that was ever fought and won by our soldiers—the great battle of Inkermann in the Crimea. (Applause.) He almost thought he could remember the announcement of that victory on the placard in big letters, with the enormous number of killed in which like a little boy he rejoiced greatly— (laughter)—at the doorway of Mr. Poulson's or Mr. Haddock's shop at Market Gate. Only a little more than a fortnight ago was the anniversary of a still greater and more celebrated victory than the soldiers' fight at Inkerraann and that was the great anti never- to-be forgotten victory of Trafalgar. (Applause.) He delighted to be able to mention these two battles because they were so well commemorative of both the army and the navy. (Applause.) Mr. Pierpoint sent on to pay a tribute to the patriotism of the Warrington volunteers and militia who responded so nobly to their country's call at the time of the Boer war. Everybody, perhaps, was too apt in considering the sdoings of the foie() to pay all the honour to the great generals and admirals who won the victories. Great honour was due to them, but let us sometimes think of the men who fought in the ranks, the non-commissioned officers and the Privates, and he thought he might broach that senti- ment best jutlig lines : Great 11. the facile conqueror, But haply he who wounded sore, Breathless, unhorsed, and covered o'er With blood and sweat, Sinks foiled.but fighting ever more Is greater yet. (A ppla use). Sergeant-Major DELMAGE responded and ssid the thoughtfulness which prompted people in their enjoy- ment to think kindly of the sailors and soldiers of the King was highly appreciated by the members of the setvrces. They were not merely the gilded butterflies that people might think they were, but very often thinking men who knew how to appreciate the kind words spoken of them. (Applause).

'THE WARRINGTON CYCLING CLUB." MR.
