EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

MR. SEDDON, M.P. ON SII3P ASSISTANTS' GRIEVASCES.

Speaking at a shop assistants' demonstration at Liver- pool on Sunday, Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P. appealed to parents to be less ready in sending their boys and girls to work in shops. Living-in was a survival of a bygone age and had become debased. To-day it represented not moral guardianship, as of old, but racial suicide. He held in his band a book of shop rules which embraced seventy-six fines. No one less than an angel from Heaven could live half an hour without incur-ring one of these penalties. Turning to legislation, he hoped next session to see an amendment to the Truck Acts, so that all shop assistants would be paid in the current coin. It was also proposed to reduce shop hours to sixty per weak, instead of the present hours which ran up to 100. The co-operative stores worked 52 hours, and shops in the colonies 53 hours. Frades unionists had relieved 8,000 managers of radius agreements, and he was resolved that such agreements should be abolished. Parliament —like Heaven—helped those who helped themselves.

===== 0002601_19071109_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
NEWTON URBAN COUNCIL.

Mr. T. Ormand presided at the monthly meeting of this Council which was held at the Earlestown Town Hall on Monday evening. There were also present : Messrs. C. B. F. Borron, E. T. Evans, W. Thompson, F. Harwood, M. Seton, A. Tunstall, T. Ritchie, L. E. Pilkington J. Naylor, C. Colo (clerk), and J. Bowes (survsyor.)—At a meeting of the Sanitary Com- mittee, Dr. Harold E. Watkins, medical officer of health, presented his quarterly report, in which he stated that 131 births and 35 deaths were reels- tered. The birth-rate was equal to 27.2 per 1,000 against 27.03 for the last quarter. The death. rate was 7.12 per 1,000 against 10.98 for the last ' quarter. The public health during the quarter bad been very good indeed. The death-rate, 7.12 per 1,000, was the lowest he ever remembered in the township. The birth-rate, 27•2, was very slightly higher than that of last quarter, but was still low for a district such as that. There had boon rather more notifications of infections disease received, but not many. No disease had occurred in epidemic form. What diseases had occurred had not been of a very severe form, as no death had taken place in the township of an infectious disease itself.—The minutes were confirmed.—At a meeting of the same committee, it was reported that with regard to the complaints of the pollution of Newton Mere the Haydock Urban District Council had commenced the construction of sewage disposal i works to deal with the sewage in the easterly I part of their district which when completed would prevent the pollution complained of I from Haydock. The =lutes were approved.-. I At a meeting of the Higher Educeition and Technical, I Instruction Committee, held on October 21st, letter was read " from the Local Government Board, ' stating that they had been unable to Idea with the ; Council's application for sanction to the borrowing of a sum of £ll,OOO for the purpose of erecting a new technical school, in consequence of a difficulty arising from the fact that the annual charges in respect of the proposed loan would exceed the produce of a penny rate in the district : that the Board were advised that they could not sanction a loan for higher educa- tion where they knew that the resources of the District Council. under the proviso to Sect. 3 of the Education Act, 1902, would be insufficient to meet the loan charges, and it appeared to them that the arrangement proposed

with the County Council would not meet the difficulty, but that Sect. 4 of the Education (Administrative Provi- sions) Act, 1907, however, now dealt with the matter; also that it would apparently be necessary that amend- ments in the existing agreement with the Colinty Council should be made, so that a new agreement should be entered into, and the District Coincil should communicate with the County Council on the subject.— The minutes were approved.

T. HELENS.

srAcs FIRE AT RAINFORD.

A somewhat serious lire occurred o'n Sunday afternoon in the stickyard of Ar. Jo3eph Appleton's faun, Moss- lane. Red Ca, R tinford. ['ha farm, which is owned by Lord Derby, is of considor Cole extent. A large stack of unthrashed oats which stood in the centre of the stackyard surrounded by live other stacks, and also a Dutch barn, which contained about 100 tons of unthrashed oats, was seen to be on fire hy a woman living in a cottage near. Mr. Appleton was away from home, but assistance was obtained from Rainford, and the St. Helens Fire Brigade was sent for. The firemen, under the charge of Superintendent Lyon, managed to confine the outbreak to the one stack, but a large hay- rick was also somewhat damaged. The damage, which estia ste! at al) ,ut Zi9, is c )var,3l by ins.traii:9. ST. HELENS ELECTIONS. DEFEATED CANDIDATE'S THREAT.

The municipal elections at St. Helens went off very quietly. Mr. M'Cormich, the defeated Labour candidate in Central Ward, complains very bitter of the literature sent out against him by the Opposition. Mr. Morris Darley, who got only three votes in the South Windle Ward, declared on Saturday that he was about to take proceedings against - two townsmen, and he also threatened to have the election declared void on the charge of bribery and corruption. In his address to the electors after the poll, he said he had proved -it. Helens was not ungodly and unholy. "We are better off," he added," than Sodom and Gomorrah. There are three straight, square chaps who voted for Old Tom.'" ST. HELENS MAYORALTY.

An informal meeting of the St. Helens Town Council was held on Thursday afternoon to deal with the question of the mayoralty, when a requisition was presented to Alderman H. Martin, J.P., asking him to take the office of mayor. Mr. Martin has already served in the office four years, in 1894 and 1895 and in 1904 and 1905. He has been a member of the Council since 1888, and has done yeoman service on the principal committees of the Council. Colonel R. Pilkington and the late Sir David Gamble are the only other members of the Council who have been mayor during four years, and by again accepting the position Alder- man Martin has set up a local record. The Mayor Alderman C, J. Bishop, in presenting the requisition, said that although it had been got up very hurriedly the requisition had been signed by a large number of members of the Council. Mr. Martin had fulfilled the duties of the office during several years with so much satisfaction to the Council and the townspeople that they felt every confidence in asking him to relieve them from the difficulty they were in, about the mayoralty. The members of the Council would be deeply indebted to him if he would again consent to be Mayor.—Alderman Martin said he felt highly honoured by the request to again become Mayor. Under all the circumstances, he felt it right to accede to the request. He loved the work of the municipality, and had taken a very deep interest in all that pertained to the well-being of the town. His one desire had been to serve his fellow-townsmen to the best of his ability, and if it was the wish of the Council that be should again occupy the position of mayor he would be very happy to place his services at their disposal. (Applause.) Alderman iVfassey,who was supported by Sir Christopher Gamble, Alderman W. W. Pilkington, Alderman R. Pilkington, and other members, expressed the satis- faction of the Council that Alderman Martin should have responded to a sense of duty and have accepted the office. He set an example to many others in the Council by his desirs to render all the service he could and his efforts to promote , the best interests of the town.

THE ORIENTAL QUESTION. ' • Letter From a Warrington tan in British Columbia.

Wo have received the following letter from Mr. J. C. Beswick, formerly of Warrington, and brother of Mr. W. R. Beswick, 47, Bewsey-street : Y.n.c.A., Vancouver, British Columbia, Canada. October 10th, 1907. Sir,—l write with the earnest request for these lines to be given space in your paper, on that trying problem, which is the worry and menace of our Western Province British Columbia, viz., Asiatic immigration. My appeal therefore, is to every right-thinking Britisher. First, here is a rich, beautiful province, and I might safely say, the richest in the whole of his Majesty's Empire, and how beautiful, one cannot realise without having seen. Yet how indifferent is our Government to the appeal of the populace out here for a fairer treatment in the adjustment of this appalling flood of Orientals. Think of it, you who sit at home. Perhaps one of your sons is here, trying with all honest purpose to make a way for himself in life, when on arriving here, he finds that out of a population of some 70,000, ten thousand are Orientals. It looks to me as if all pride were lost in the Anglo-Saxon race, the mingling of which is dis- gusting to think of. But to get back to the point. Before he starts from the homelaud,our hopeful fellow- Britisher has had all the bright glow of a "Golder. West" well depicted to him, for this is all planned by the cunning agent of the largo shipping and railway monopoly, which has only in view its own interests (and those pretty large) for it must be remembered that the rates to come out here are cut, but when our hopeful brother gets here he finds its another thing to get out, and further he finds that he has not been informed of the competition in labour put against him by the unfair permitting of cheap Asiatic labour, which teems in at the back door of the dominion. Think of it, friends, the picture it would make for you, could you see 900 Hindoos streaming down the central thorough- fare of your town, as they did only to-day in our city, and it ninst be remembered that this is only one batch in the last month or so, for the Japs are continually pourieg in awl here's my fellow-Britisher who has to look on and behold with a heavy heart what he has to compete against. Many I have spoken to, myself, who have honestly tried their best to get a position, but weary and footsore from tramping around, are about giving in, when someone whispers to them" Why don't you try the sawmills ; take anything for a start." Away I they start and reach the said mills only to see peeping over their saw benches and machines rows of brown faces, and they wonder whether they are in Tokio or Hong Kong. Now I'm speaking from experience, having seen this for myself. I'm a Lancashire man, but it makes my blood boil to see the preference shown to these Orientals, over those of our own race. Is it fair play to we who have to work for our livelihood ? And it's only natural that we wish the splendid develop- ment of this splendid province to be carried on by white labour. It's absolutely impossible for a Britisher with any pride or self-resTiect to try and live decently on the pay an Oriental receives. Here's another thing. If I am not mistaken, the report last year was that 65 per cent of the industries of this province was lumbering, and yet because a white man kicks - agajnst the resources of oar province being turned into wages by too large a proportion of pigtails, Scc., he's told he wants too much.

Now, sir, it's pretty hard when you find yourself up against this work problem and know that you must work to ear,. And everybody cannot work in the cities —what few there are out West now, Air. Editor. I feel that little as I know of administration I ought to speak. An Act, which I understand would help us a little, was placed before the Lieutenant Governor here some two or three years ago. He absolutely refuses to sign. I plead with all true Britishers to give this their careful attention, and may we soon see this wealthy province held for those who may, in future, leave the old shores for a new home, for it's a beautiful land. I love the dear old soil, but ruy heart's wrapped up in our lovely province and it is worthy of guarding with a jealous eye.—Yours truly, _

(gigned) J. C. BESWICK.

HUMBER IMPROVEMENTS FOR 1908.

We naturally look to the great Humber firm, the largest motor manufacturing house in the kingdom, for considerable improvements, but the 1908 models to he displayed at Stand 165, Olympia, on Monday next, will more than realize expectations,even though the Humber cars have already attained such a pinnacle of excellence that, to the ordinary motorist, the directions for improvement are very far from apparent. The car 'which has carried off the chief honours of the year. 'winning gold medals in every one of the international reliability trials, winning the International heavy car race of the Royal Antomobile Club, one of the only two cars to finish in the tourist trophy contest, to say nothing of many other splendid performances in open competition, can fairly claim to have led the van of motor improvement in 1907, and obviously, Humber, Limited, are preparing to maintain their proud position unassailable. In many respects the Humber display at Olympia will be remarkable. Six models will be shown in all.

MARRIAGE AT THELWALL

Williamson —Bleckly.

All Saints' Church, Thelwall, was the/scene nf an exceedingly pretty wedding on Tuesday afternoon,when Miss Helen Maud Bleckly, only daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Bleckly, Thelwall Lea, was married to Mr. George I Royds Williamson, third son of the Rev. T. Pym and Mrs. Williamson, Thelwall Vicarage. The bride and bridegroom are both well known and highly esteemed in the district and a large congregation assembled to witness the ceremony. The chancel of the church was beautifully decorated. with white chrysanthemums and foliage plants. The service was choral,the choir being in attendance and leading the singing of the hymns "How welcome was the call," and 0 perfect love, all human thought transcending." The officiating clergy were the Rev. T. Pyro Williamson, father of the bridegronm, and the Rev. Wilfrid J. Stanton. Mr. W. B. Cook presided at the organ, and while the guests wore assembling and at the close of the service played the following choice selections : "March in E Flat" (Lefebure-Wely) ; "Triumphal March " (Sainton-Dolby) ; "The Golden Trumpets" (Fowles) ; " Farfait Armour" (Hime) ; " Offertoire in F " (Lefebure-Wely) ; "Marche Jubilante " (Cooper) ; " Angelic Voices ' (Batiste) ; "Meditation in E" (Steane) ; " Elsa's Bridal Song " from " Lohengrin," Mendelssohn's Wedding March; the "Triumphal March " from "Naaman "(Costa); and the "Trumpet March " (Jude). The bride, who was given by her father, was beautifully attired in white satin trimmed with Carrickmacross lace, a veil of embro:dered Brussels net, and sliver slippers. Her bouquet consisted of a sheaf of lilium harrissi, tied with silver ribbon, the gift of the bridegroom. There wete four bridesmaids—Miss Hilda de Angelis and Miss E. Bleckly (cousins of the bride), Miss J. Cattley (cousin of the bridegroom), and Miss Edmondson They wore very pretty dresses of pastel blue face cloth, trimmed with Guipure lace and brown silk, and brown hats trimmed with feathers. They carried posey bouquets of shaded brown chrysanthemums, tied with brown ribbon, and these together with Carrickmacross lace handkerchiefs, were the gifts of the bridegroom. Master lan Jones-Parry (nephew and godson of the bridegoom), who wore a dainty man-of-war suit, with a gold pin given to him by the bridegroom, was in attendance as page. Mr. F. H, Williamson (brother of the bridegroom) discharged the duties of best man.

After the ceremony a reception was held at Thelwall Lea, over 250 guests attending. Later the bride and bridegroom, after receiving the hearty congratulations and good wishes of their friends, left for their honey- moon tour. The bride travelled in a dress of Hussar blue face cloth trimmed with astrachan, the vest being braided with gold. She also wore an astrachan stole, the gift of the bridegrocm.

__,.____ ROYAL COURT THEATRE. " Sweet Nancy."

There has been a capital attraction for habiturs of the Royal Court Theatre this week, and as a conse- quence large " houses "have been the order. The War- rington public always appreciates a good thing,and Mist Annie Hughes and her company in "Sweet Nancy,, have been accorded a warm welcome. "Sweet Nancy is a comedy in three acts by Robert Buchanan and a better thing of its kind we have not seen. It abounds in fun and high-class humour, while the pathetic side is introduced with an appropriateness which enhances the quality of the comedy. The story is quite a simPle one. Sweet Nancy, the second daughter of Mr. Grill, is wooed and won at the age of nineteen by General Sir Roger Tempest, a college fellow of her father's - The scene changes.to -Tempest Manor, where nothing very remarkable occurs. Yet the interest is alwaYS keenly maintained. True, there is a mischief-making grass-widow, a foolish boy, and a young man who makes love to Nancy while her husband is away at the South African war. Nancy is wholly innocent and imagines that Musgrave is attracted by her sister Barbara All ends well eventually except for poor Barbara,whose affections have been trifled with, but who shows a fine heroism and spirit of self-sacrifice in the last act. Miss Annie Hughes is a very pretty and accomplishe4 actress, and her interpretation of the title role cou i ld not be improved upon. She is artless, artful, tender, and dignified by turns and presents a fascinating studY. The Grey family, Algernon' Bobby, Barbara, Nancy, Teresa, and Jane are capital fun together. A more real, human, and natural English family we never met- Mischisf and merriment seem to be their watchwoids, and even a tyrant of a father fails to suLdue their exuberance. Mr. Rupert Lister makes 9 very manly and soldierly Sir Roger Tempest, while Miss Mabel Trevor presents a finished pourtrayal of the grass widow, Mrs. Huntley. . A special word of praise should also be said of the east pleasant acting of Miss Vera Pole as Barbara, while the boys (Nle,ssrs. H. Douglas Greet, and Alia Chumley) are typically English and lad-like. It is really difficult to differentiate in so strong a company, the oast of which is : General Sir Roger Tempest Mr, Rupert Lister Frank Musgrave, Sir Roger's iVard...Mr. Charles lisdas 'Nicholas O'Shea, Sir Roger's Servant Mr. geginald F. Beach Mr. Grey, an old College Fellow of Sir Roger's Mr. Evelyn Vernoe• Algernon 1 Mr. Gray's) Mr. H. Douglas Great Bobby f Sons f lr. Alick Chumiey Mrs. Grey, Mr. Grey's wife Miss Mary Duggan Barbara Grey Miss Vera Pole Nancy Grey, Sweet Nancy Miss Annie Hughes , Teresa Grey„ Tow Tow Miss Dorothy 51ackerizie Jane Grey, the Brat ' Miss May Collier Mrs. Huntley, a Grass Widow... Miss Mabel Trevor Alice Burton, Parlourmaid at Tempest Mannor Miss Kate Mile Each evening the comedy has been preceded bY dramatic curtain raiser entitled "After many years' from the pen of Miss Hughes herself. It is smartly written and just as smartly acted by Mr. Rupert Lister as Phillip Carruthers, a blackmailer ' • Mr. Charles Esdale as Bill Burke, a burglar; Miss Mabel Trevor Mrs. Vivian, a Society lady; and Miss Vera Pole as Eliza Dent, Mrs. Vivian's maid.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. Next week Mr. T. Edward Ward's Company will'. appear in the romantic. drama "Second to None: story of the Royal Scots Grays" by Walter Howard. As most of our readers will know, Mr. Howard is a.verJ successful plavwrieht, two of his dramas "Her against the World" and "The midnight Wedding , having run very successfully at the Lyceum. " Senol3`._ to None" is his latest play and has airmdy proved highly popular. The company which will present it tit the RoyaliCourt Theatre, Warrington' is a strong one; _ Miss Ward, who will take the part of "Nell Gre3stone is a well known actress, having sustained the leading roles in "Her wedding day" and" Man and wife." ' Jerrold Heather, who will appear as "Harry NVilding,_ is also a favourite, one of his principal impersonatioos being that o_f Cromwell iu "Fr the King."
